Table 1
How Modern AI Works
1. Q: What is the central idea behind the evolution of modern artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
Artificial intelligence has evolved from rule-based programs and specialized prediction systems into models capable of generating language, images, code, and other forms of content. This development did not follow a straight path. Periods of impressive progress were followed by disappointment when systems failed to meet expectations. Modern generative AI represents a significant change because people can interact with it through ordinary language rather than relying exclusively on specialized software interfaces. However, impressive outputs do not automatically establish genuine understanding, reliability, or independent intelligence. The practical challenge is to understand both what these systems can accomplish and where human judgment remains essential.
Study instruction:
- Create a timeline distinguishing early symbolic AI, predictive machine learning, Transformer-based language models, and contemporary multimodal systems.
Key point:
- AI progress combines technological advances with recurring questions about capability and reliability.
- Practical usefulness must be evaluated separately from the appearance of intelligence.
Source links:
- The Founding and Future of AI — Dartmouth
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
2. Q: How should artificial intelligence, machine learning, and generative AI be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Artificial intelligence is the broad field concerned with creating systems that perform tasks associated with intelligent behavior. Machine learning is an approach within AI in which systems learn patterns from data rather than relying exclusively on explicitly written rules. Generative AI describes systems designed to produce new content, including text, images, audio, video, and computer code. These categories overlap: a generative language model is an AI system that uses machine learning. Predictive models and generative models may use related statistical techniques, but their objectives differ. A predictive system might estimate which products will sell next month, while a generative system might draft a marketing plan using sales information as context.
Study instruction:
- Classify five practical applications as primarily predictive, generative, rule-based, or hybrid, and explain any overlap.
Key point:
- AI, machine learning, and generative AI are related but not interchangeable terms.
- The intended output helps determine which AI approach is appropriate.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative AI Profile — NIST
3. Q: What can the history of early mechanical demonstrations and artificial intelligence teach us about judging technology?
A: Show answer
Historical demonstrations show that people can overestimate a machine's underlying capabilities when they see an impressive performance. A device may appear intelligent because of hidden human involvement, carefully controlled conditions, or a narrowly defined task. Conversely, an unusual demonstration may reveal a genuinely useful technical advance that deserves further investigation. The appropriate response is neither automatic enthusiasm nor blanket skepticism. Ask what the machine actually did, what assistance it received, what conditions were controlled, and whether the result can be independently reproduced. This approach applies equally to historical mechanical demonstrations, modern AI product launches, and impressive model-generated outputs.
Study instruction:
- Select an AI demonstration and document its task, operating conditions, human involvement, independent evaluation, and remaining uncertainties.
Key point:
- A demonstration establishes performance under particular conditions, not universal capability.
- Independent testing is necessary to distinguish a persuasive presentation from a dependable system.
Source links:
- Computing Machinery and Intelligence — A. M. Turing, Oxford Academic
- The Founding and Future of AI — Dartmouth
4. Q: Why have expectations about artificial intelligence repeatedly risen and fallen?
A: Show answer
AI research periodically attracts intense attention when a new technique produces surprising results or promises substantial economic benefits. Expectations may then exceed what the technology can reliably deliver. Limited computing resources, inadequate data, difficult real-world conditions, or fundamental weaknesses can prevent early demonstrations from becoming practical solutions. Disappointment may reduce funding and enthusiasm, even when useful research continues. Organizations should therefore evaluate technology according to measurable outcomes rather than promotional forecasts. A disciplined investment decision identifies the business problem, establishes a baseline, tests the proposed system under realistic conditions, and specifies what evidence would justify wider implementation.
Study instruction:
- Develop an AI pilot proposal with measurable benefits, operating costs, failure criteria, and conditions for scaling or stopping the project.
Key point:
- Technological progress and commercial expectations do not always advance at the same pace.
- Investment decisions should depend on demonstrated value and manageable risk.
Source links:
- The Founding and Future of AI — Dartmouth
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
5. Q: What is predictive AI, and how does it create value for organizations?
A: Show answer
Predictive AI analyzes patterns in existing data to estimate likely outcomes. Examples include forecasting demand, estimating credit risk, predicting equipment failure, identifying suspicious transactions, and recommending products. Its economic value comes from improving decisions that would otherwise depend on incomplete information or manual estimates. A demand-forecasting system might combine historical sales, seasonal patterns, weather conditions, and local events to estimate future inventory requirements. However, a statistically accurate forecast is not automatically a good decision. Managers must also consider the cost of errors, the consequences of overestimating or underestimating demand, and whether the operating environment differs from the historical data.
Study instruction:
- Choose a forecasting problem and specify its target variable, historical inputs, evaluation metric, decision owner, and consequences of incorrect predictions.
Key point:
- Predictive systems estimate outcomes to support decisions.
- Business value depends on how predictions influence actions, not merely on statistical accuracy.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
6. Q: What is supervised learning, and why does it depend on labeled data?
A: Show answer
Supervised learning trains a model using examples that pair inputs with known target outputs. For instance, a system that classifies customer complaints might learn from historical messages labeled by their complaint categories. The model adjusts its parameters to reduce differences between its predictions and the provided answers. The quality of the resulting system depends on the quality, representativeness, and consistency of those examples. If labels contain historical biases or fail to reflect new conditions, the model may reproduce those problems. Organizations should document how training labels were defined, test for inconsistent classification, and monitor performance on data that was not used to train the model.
Study instruction:
- Design a small supervised-learning dataset and identify the input fields, target labels, labeling rules, and independent test sample.
Key point:
- Supervised learning learns from examples paired with target answers.
- Incorrect or unrepresentative labels can undermine model performance.
Source links:
- Machine Learning Crash Course — Google for Developers
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
7. Q: How does generative AI differ from conventional predictive systems?
A: Show answer
Conventional predictive systems often produce a defined output, such as a probability, category, or numerical forecast. Generative systems construct new content based on patterns learned during training and the instructions or information provided at inference time. A predictive model might estimate the probability that a supplier will deliver late; a generative model might draft a supplier-risk briefing using delivery records and other supplied information. The distinction is not absolute because generative systems also make statistical predictions, and some workflows combine prediction and generation. Organizations should choose the approach that best fits the task rather than assuming that the newest or most flexible model is always superior.
Study instruction:
- Compare a conventional classifier and a language model for the same operational problem. Evaluate accuracy, explainability, cost, flexibility, and review requirements.
Key point:
- Generative AI creates content, whereas conventional predictive systems often estimate predefined outcomes.
- Hybrid workflows can combine numerical prediction with narrative explanation.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
8. Q: What problem did the Transformer architecture help address in language processing?
A: Show answer
Earlier language-processing approaches often struggled to preserve important relationships across long passages or process sequences efficiently. The Transformer architecture introduced an attention-based approach that lets a model calculate relationships between elements in a sequence. This enables the model to use contextual information when processing language rather than relying only on simple local patterns or sequential processing. Transformers also support efficient parallel computation during training. Their development helped make large-scale language modeling substantially more practical, although architecture alone does not guarantee factual accuracy, sound reasoning, or safe behavior. Data quality, training procedures, model size, and later evaluations remain important.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between processing each word mainly in sequence and using attention to relate multiple parts of a passage.
Key point:
- Attention helps models use contextual relationships in language.
- Transformers were an important architectural advance, not a complete solution to every AI limitation.
Source links:
- Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani et al.
9. Q: What does an attention mechanism do in a language model?
A: Show answer
Attention assigns different degrees of importance to relationships among elements in a sequence when computing a representation. For example, understanding a sentence may require connecting a pronoun with an earlier noun or distinguishing the intended meaning of a word from its surrounding context. Attention allows a Transformer to incorporate information from relevant positions instead of treating every surrounding word as equally informative. Multiple attention heads can learn different patterns of relationships. Nevertheless, attention weights should not automatically be treated as a complete explanation of the model's reasoning. The architecture describes part of how information is processed, not a transparent record of every factor behind an answer.
Study instruction:
- Create a sentence containing a pronoun, an ambiguous word, and a distant contextual clue. Explain which relationships a language model would need to resolve its meaning.
Key point:
- Context can change how a word or phrase should be interpreted.
- Attention helps process contextual relationships but does not guarantee correct interpretation.
Source links:
- Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani et al.
10. Q: What is a token, and why is next-token prediction central to many large language models?
A: Show answer
A token is a unit of text processed by a language model. Depending on the tokenization method, it may represent a complete word, part of a word, punctuation, or another text fragment. During generation, an autoregressive language model uses the preceding context to estimate a probability distribution over possible next tokens. A selection is made from that distribution, and the process repeats until the response ends or another stopping condition is reached. This mechanism can produce paragraphs, code, summaries, and explanations because the model has learned many statistical and contextual patterns from training. However, predicting plausible continuations does not independently verify whether the resulting statements are factually correct.
Study instruction:
- Take a short sentence and describe how several plausible next tokens could lead to different continuations.
Key point:
- Tokens are the units that a language model processes and generates.
- Next-token prediction is a generation mechanism, not a guarantee of truth.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani et al.
11. Q: Why can the same prompt produce different answers from a language model?
A: Show answer
A language model may assign nonzero probabilities to several plausible continuations. The generation procedure can sample among those alternatives or use another selection strategy. When variation is permitted, repeated requests may produce different wording, examples, levels of detail, or conclusions. This is useful for brainstorming and creative work, where several acceptable answers may exist. It can be problematic for standardized reporting, repeated calculations, or controlled workflows that require consistency. Temperature and other generation settings can influence variation, but they do not independently establish factual reliability. Where consistency matters, use precise instructions, controlled inputs, structured output requirements, and independent checks.
Study instruction:
- Submit the same well-defined prompt several times. Compare factual claims, numerical results, format, and unnecessary variation.
Key point:
- Variation can arise from the generation method and the existence of multiple plausible continuations.
- Repeatability should be tested whenever consistent outputs are operationally important.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
12. Q: What happens during pretraining of a large language model?
A: Show answer
Pretraining exposes a model to large collections of data and adjusts its parameters so that it becomes better at predicting the target tokens in the training task. Preparing the data commonly involves collecting permitted sources, filtering unsuitable material, removing or managing duplicates, and converting examples into a format appropriate for the model. The model then undergoes repeated optimization across many training examples. Through this process, it can learn statistical associations, grammar, styles, factual patterns, and representations useful for completing many different tasks. The resulting model is not automatically a reliable assistant: it may reproduce harmful patterns, make errors, or respond in ways that do not satisfy human expectations.
Study instruction:
- Map a hypothetical language-model development process from data preparation through pretraining and evaluation. Identify a quality control at each stage.
Key point:
- Pretraining builds general model capabilities from large-scale training data.
- Pretrained capability does not, by itself, ensure safe or instruction-following behavior.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback — Ouyang et al.
13. Q: What are model parameters or weights, and how do they influence outputs?
A: Show answer
Model parameters are numerical values adjusted during training to influence how information is transformed and how predictions are produced. Large language models may contain very large numbers of parameters, but parameter count alone does not determine quality. Architecture, training data, optimization, inference methods, and post-training also matter. Learned parameters encode patterns that help a model generalize across examples; they should not be understood simply as a database of neatly stored facts. The model may produce a correct answer, an inaccurate recollection, or a plausible invention depending on the task and context. Understanding this distinction helps explain why a fluent system can still make factual mistakes.
Study instruction:
- Explain how learning patterns from examples differs from storing a conventional database record that can be retrieved exactly.
Key point:
- Weights encode learned transformations and relationships that influence generation.
- A large parameter count is not proof of dependable knowledge or reasoning.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani et al.
14. Q: Why are large language models expensive to train?
A: Show answer
Training requires substantial computation to process data, calculate model outputs, measure training errors, and update large collections of parameters repeatedly. High-performance accelerators, memory, networking, storage, engineering expertise, and energy contribute to the overall cost. Costs also arise from experimentation, unsuccessful training runs, safety evaluation, and later refinement. These expenses help explain why only some organizations develop the largest frontier models, while smaller organizations may specialize in narrower models or adapt existing systems. A model's full cost of ownership also includes inference, maintenance, integration, governance, and human review rather than only the original training expenditure.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a model-selection comparison that includes training or licensing, usage, integration, monitoring, security, and human-review costs.
Key point:
- Large-scale training requires significant computational and operational resources.
- The cheapest model to access may not be the cheapest system to operate reliably.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
15. Q: Why does training-data quality matter as much as data quantity?
A: Show answer
A large dataset can contain inaccurate claims, duplicated material, outdated information, biased examples, poor writing, or content that is inappropriate for the intended task. Increasing its size does not automatically correct these weaknesses. Data quality depends on relevance, coverage, consistency, provenance, representativeness, and appropriate handling of sensitive or protected material. Developers must make decisions about what to include, exclude, filter, and evaluate. Organizations using AI should also remember that general training data may not contain current internal policies or the latest operational facts. Supplying validated, task-specific context can improve relevance, but that information must still be checked for completeness and accuracy.
Study instruction:
- Draft a training-data quality checklist covering provenance, licensing, duplicates, sensitive information, representation, accuracy, and update frequency.
Key point:
- Scale cannot compensate for every weakness in data quality.
- Data preparation and governance influence model behavior and downstream risks.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
16. Q: What ethical and legal questions arise when copyrighted material is included in AI training data?
A: Show answer
Training-data practices raise questions about authorization, licensing, privacy, compensation, attribution, and the potential reproduction of protected expression. The legal treatment of particular training practices varies with jurisdiction, facts, applicable exceptions, contracts, and evolving court decisions. It is therefore inappropriate to assume either that every use is lawful or that every use is prohibited. Organizations developing or deploying AI should review the rights attached to their data, establish acceptable-use rules, assess vendor terms, and maintain records of relevant decisions. Users should also avoid assuming that an AI-generated output is automatically free of intellectual-property risks merely because a model produced it.
Study instruction:
- Create a risk register for using AI-generated material in a commercial or educational project. Include licensing, ownership, attribution, confidentiality, and human review.
Key point:
- Training-data and output-related legal questions depend on the applicable facts and law.
- Responsible use requires rights review and clear organizational policies.
Source links:
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
17. Q: How can a language model reproduce bias or misinformation from its training data?
A: Show answer
A model learns statistical patterns from the examples and signals available during development. When those examples contain stereotypes, inaccurate assertions, uneven representation, or misleading associations, the model may produce outputs reflecting similar patterns. Other biases can arise from data selection, model design, optimization objectives, and later feedback. The risks are not eliminated by making responses more polished or friendly. Evaluation should test the relevant groups, languages, contexts, and decision types that the system will encounter. When bias is detected, organizations should identify its source, consider the potential harm, introduce proportionate safeguards, and monitor whether the changes improve outcomes without creating new problems.
Study instruction:
- Design a small bias assessment for a proposed AI-assisted screening or recommendation process. Specify test cases, outcome measures, and escalation criteria.
Key point:
- Learned patterns may reproduce or amplify problematic features of the underlying data and development process.
- Bias testing must reflect the intended application and possible consequences.
Source links:
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
18. Q: Why does pretraining alone not make a language model a reliable assistant?
A: Show answer
Pretraining primarily develops the ability to predict and generate patterns from training examples. A model in this state may not follow instructions consistently, recognize safety boundaries, distinguish helpful answers from harmful ones, or organize its response according to a user's needs. It may reproduce offensive or misleading material because its initial training objective does not necessarily encode the preferences and restrictions expected of an assistant. Additional development stages are commonly used to improve instruction following, response quality, and safety. Even after these stages, testing is necessary because improvements in desirable behavior do not eliminate every failure mode.
Study instruction:
- Compare the requirements of a general text-completion model with those of an assistant intended to support customer service or research.
Key point:
- General language capability and useful assistant behavior are different objectives.
- Additional alignment and evaluation are needed for dependable deployment.
Source links:
- Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback — Ouyang et al.
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
19. Q: What is fine-tuning, and how can it improve a model for a specific task?
A: Show answer
Fine-tuning adjusts a previously trained model using additional training examples or optimization objectives. It can help a model adopt a particular response format, follow instructions more consistently, or perform better in a specialized application. A customer-service organization, for example, may use approved examples of customer questions and suitable responses to improve a model's handling of common requests. The result depends on the quality and coverage of the examples and the evaluation process. Fine-tuning does not automatically give the model verified access to the latest information, guarantee factual accuracy, or replace operational safeguards.
Study instruction:
- Propose a small fine-tuning dataset for a narrow workplace task. Define the examples, quality criteria, held-out test data, and failure thresholds.
Key point:
- Fine-tuning adapts a pretrained model to additional examples or objectives.
- Task adaptation must be evaluated against realistic requirements.
Source links:
- Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback — Ouyang et al.
- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
20. Q: What is reinforcement learning from human feedback, and what problem does it address?
A: Show answer
Reinforcement learning from human feedback, or RLHF, is one approach to aligning model behavior with human preferences. Human evaluators compare or rate outputs according to defined criteria, and those judgments contribute to a training process that encourages more desirable responses. This can improve instruction following, helpfulness, and the handling of certain undesirable outputs. The method introduces its own challenges: human judgments can be inconsistent, evaluation criteria can be incomplete, and the resulting optimization may encourage behaviors that score well without fully satisfying the underlying goal. Human feedback is therefore a useful signal rather than a universal definition of truth or ethical correctness.
Study instruction:
- Create evaluation criteria for comparing two AI responses, distinguishing factual accuracy, helpfulness, completeness, safety, and style.
Key point:
- Human feedback helps shape how a model responds to instructions and preferences.
- The quality of the alignment process depends on the feedback, objectives, and evaluation methods.
Source links:
- Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback — Ouyang et al.
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
21. Q: How can feedback and later training make AI responses more useful without making them infallible?
A: Show answer
Feedback can identify answers that are unclear, incomplete, unsafe, or inconsistent with user preferences. Developers may use those judgments to improve the model, modify instructions, adjust training examples, or change system-level safeguards. These interventions can raise performance on some tasks but may have little effect on unrelated failure modes. Optimizing for one quality can also produce trade-offs with another. For instance, a system optimized to provide direct answers might become less likely to ask necessary clarifying questions. A proper evaluation therefore measures multiple qualities, tests unintended effects, and checks performance beyond the examples used for improvement.
Study instruction:
- Build a before-and-after evaluation plan for an AI improvement, using separate development and test cases to detect regressions.
Key point:
- Feedback is useful for iterative improvement but cannot guarantee universal correctness.
- Improvements should be tested against several independent performance and safety criteria.
Source links:
- Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback — Ouyang et al.
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
22. Q: Why can a language model appear self-aware or emotionally responsive without proving that it has subjective experience?
A: Show answer
Language models learn patterns in how people describe thoughts, beliefs, intentions, emotions, and relationships. They can reproduce those patterns convincingly, including first-person statements and emotionally appropriate language. However, the presence of such language is not sufficient evidence of consciousness, subjective experience, or independent intentions. A model may adopt a supportive tone because of its instructions and learned behavior, rather than because it experiences the emotion it describes. Users should assess the information and functionality a system provides instead of inferring inner experience from conversational fluency. Questions about machine consciousness remain distinct from practical evaluations of performance and reliability.
Study instruction:
- Compare several emotionally expressive AI responses and identify which claims describe observable system behavior and which infer unverified inner experience.
Key point:
- Humanlike communication does not establish consciousness or feelings.
- Evaluate demonstrated capabilities separately from claims about subjective experience.
Source links:
- Computing Machinery and Intelligence — A. M. Turing, Oxford Academic
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
23. Q: How can large language models generate fluent text despite having weaknesses in reasoning or factual accuracy?
A: Show answer
Language models learn complex statistical relationships that help them produce text resembling coherent human communication. Fluency is a separate quality from factual verification. A response may be grammatically correct, professionally written, and logically connected while containing a false premise, invalid inference, or unsupported conclusion. This mismatch is especially risky when users judge the answer by tone rather than evidence. A sound workflow separates drafting from verification: ask the model to generate a candidate, check factual claims against authoritative material, independently verify calculations, and review whether conclusions follow from the supplied information.
Study instruction:
- Prepare two test questions, one assessing writing quality and another assessing factual correctness, then explain why they require separate scoring criteria.
Key point:
- Fluent expression is not a reliable proxy for factual accuracy.
- Evidence and independent validation are necessary when correctness matters.
Source links:
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
24. Q: What are hallucinations or confabulations in generative AI?
A: Show answer
Hallucinations, also called confabulations in some risk-management literature, occur when a generative system produces information that is false, fabricated, internally inconsistent, or unsupported by the available evidence. Examples include invented references, nonexistent quotations, inaccurate legal claims, and fabricated explanations of events. These errors may occur because the system generates plausible continuations rather than independently verifying every proposition. More detailed prompts, retrieval from trusted sources, constrained output formats, and human review can reduce some risks, but none guarantees error-free output. For critical work, the review process should identify claims that require source confirmation and record how discrepancies are resolved.
Study instruction:
- Review a sample AI-generated report and label each factual claim as verified, unsupported, contradicted, or requiring further evidence.
Key point:
- A plausible answer can still contain fabricated information.
- Verification procedures should reflect the consequences of being wrong.
Source links:
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
25. Q: Why can an AI model perform well on a complex task but fail at a seemingly simple one?
A: Show answer
Model performance is uneven because tasks place different demands on perception, language, planning, exact calculation, memory, and the ability to follow instructions. A model may generate a complete program that performs a complex activity while making a basic mistake in a separate factual or logical question. Generating plausible code and proving that code is correct are distinct tasks. Likewise, explaining a mathematical method does not guarantee that a specific calculation is correct. Testing should therefore reflect the precise operation being delegated, including ordinary cases, boundary cases, and realistic exceptions.
Study instruction:
- Design a task-specific test suite containing ordinary examples, edge cases, deliberately misleading inputs, and independently verified expected outputs.
Key point:
- Capability in one task does not guarantee capability in another.
- Evaluation should target the actual work product and failure conditions.
Source links:
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
26. Q: What are the limitations of using standardized tests or benchmark scores to evaluate AI?
A: Show answer
Benchmarks provide useful comparative evidence when their design and limitations are understood. However, high scores may not establish dependable real-world performance. A model could have encountered similar material during training, benefit from favorable test design, or perform poorly when tasks differ from the benchmark. Scores also may conceal differences in reliability across subgroups, languages, or operating conditions. Organizations should use multiple evaluation methods, include realistic workflow tests, and document whether the benchmark resembles the intended application. Where feasible, test data should be separated from training and development data, and results should be independently reproducible.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a hypothetical AI benchmark by examining task relevance, data leakage, coverage, scoring method, repeatability, and real-world validity.
Key point:
- Benchmark results are evidence about tested conditions, not universal guarantees.
- Realistic independent testing is necessary before deployment.
Source links:
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
27. Q: What are emergent capabilities, and why should claims about them be interpreted carefully?
A: Show answer
Emergent capabilities are behaviors that become apparent as a model or system scales or changes, sometimes showing up sharply under particular evaluation methods. Such performance may be surprising because the system's development objective did not explicitly program a visible, step-by-step solution for the task. However, whether a capability is genuinely emergent depends on how it is measured, the task design, and how performance changes with scale. Researchers may disagree about the mechanisms behind particular results. Practical evaluation should therefore distinguish observed behavior from hypotheses about why it occurs and avoid treating an unexpected result as proof of humanlike understanding or general intelligence.
Study instruction:
- Select a reported capability and separate the observed test result, proposed explanation, alternative explanations, and evidence needed to distinguish them.
Key point:
- Unexpected performance can be real while its underlying explanation remains uncertain.
- Claims about emergence require careful measurement and reproducibility.
Source links:
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
28. Q: How do AI image-generation models differ from language models?
A: Show answer
Language models generate text tokens, whereas image-generation systems learn visual patterns and use methods designed to synthesize image content. Diffusion models commonly begin with noise and iteratively transform it toward an image consistent with learned patterns and the conditioning information supplied to the system, such as a text prompt. Training approaches can use images with descriptive text or other conditioning signals. Both language and image models rely on learned representations, but their generation procedures and error patterns differ. An image can appear realistic without depicting a real event, just as fluent writing can describe something that never happened.
Study instruction:
- Compare a generated image with the original prompt. Identify accurate elements, omitted requirements, invented details, and risks if the image is presented as authentic evidence.
Key point:
- Diffusion models use iterative denoising to generate visual content.
- Realistic appearance does not establish that an image depicts a real event.
Source links:
- Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models — Ho, Jain, and Abbeel
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
29. Q: What does multimodal AI mean, and why does it matter?
A: Show answer
Multimodal AI processes or generates more than one type of information, such as text, images, audio, or video. A multimodal assistant might interpret a diagram, summarize a meeting recording, extract information from a document image, or connect a visual observation with a written explanation. Such capabilities expand the range of tasks that can be supported through a single interface. They also introduce additional risks, including incorrect interpretation of images, missing details in audio, confusion about spatial relationships, or failures when different input types conflict. Evaluation must separately test the relevant modalities and the way information is combined across them.
Study instruction:
- Design a workflow that combines a document image with written instructions. Specify how extracted details will be checked against the original image before a decision is made.
Key point:
- Multimodal systems integrate capabilities across different forms of information.
- Each modality and the interaction between modalities require testing.
Source links:
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
30. Q: How should narrow or specialized models be compared with frontier models?
A: Show answer
Specialized models may be designed to handle a limited set of tasks efficiently, whereas frontier models generally target broad capability across diverse activities. A specialized model may be preferable for predictable, repetitive workloads where cost, latency, or a narrow performance requirement dominates. A frontier model may offer greater flexibility for tasks involving ambiguous instructions, varied documents, or multiple kinds of reasoning. Neither category is automatically better for every application. Model selection should consider output quality, reliability, security, integration requirements, inference costs, vendor dependence, and the consequences of an error. A controlled pilot should compare candidates on the actual tasks the organization needs to perform.
Study instruction:
- Build a model-selection matrix for one workplace use case. Include accuracy, consistency, speed, cost, privacy, maintainability, and human oversight.
Key point:
- Model choice depends on the use case and operational constraints.
- Broader capability may be useful, but it does not remove the need for task-specific evaluation.
Source links:
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
31. Q: Why can a language model's reasoning ability be difficult to assess from its explanations alone?
A: Show answer
A model may produce a convincing explanation that does not accurately describe how its answer was generated, or it may give a correct answer for an unreliable reason. Natural-language explanations are outputs that also need evaluation. To assess reasoning, test whether the answer follows from the supplied premises, whether calculations can be reproduced, whether alternatives have been considered, and whether conclusions survive small changes in the input. Independent verification is particularly valuable when the task involves logic, numerical analysis, or complicated instructions. The aim is not to infer hidden internal processes from persuasive prose but to establish that the result meets the required standard of correctness.
Study instruction:
- Give the model a reasoning problem with a known solution, independently solve it, and compare the answer, intermediate steps, assumptions, and sensitivity to changed inputs.
Key point:
- An articulate explanation does not automatically validate the reasoning behind a conclusion.
- Test correctness using reproducible evidence and independent methods.
Source links:
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
32. Q: What practical prompting techniques help produce more useful AI outputs?
A: Show answer
Effective instructions define the task, intended audience, context, constraints, desired format, and criteria for a successful result. Supplying relevant background reduces ambiguity, while examples can clarify expectations. Breaking a complex request into stages makes it easier to review intermediate results and identify errors. For research tasks, ask the model to distinguish verified facts from assumptions and to provide sources that can be checked. For structured tasks, specify required fields and validation rules. Prompting improves the conditions under which the system operates, but a well-written prompt cannot guarantee accuracy, safety, or completeness.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a vague instruction into a structured prompt containing objective, context, constraints, output format, evaluation criteria, and verification steps.
Key point:
- Clear instructions and relevant context improve task definition.
- Prompt quality complements, rather than replaces, independent checking.

- How ChatGPT and Our Foundation Models Are Developed — OpenAI
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
33. Q: How should AI outputs be verified before they are used in professional work?
A: Show answer
Verification should reflect the type and significance of the output. Numerical work should be recalculated or checked against trusted systems. Factual claims should be compared with authoritative sources. Code should be executed in a suitable test environment and reviewed for security and correctness. Summaries should be compared with their source materials, and generated images should not be presented as documentary evidence without authentication. Reviewers should pay particular attention to unsupported assumptions, missing information, invented sources, contradictory statements, and unapproved changes. For important decisions, retain the source documents, review evidence, corrections, and approval record so the final conclusion can be reconstructed.
Study instruction:
- Create a verification checklist for an AI-generated report and test it on a sample containing numerical claims, citations, and recommendations.
Key point:
- Different kinds of outputs require different validation methods.
- High-impact decisions require stronger evidence and documented review.

- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
- GPT-4 Research — OpenAI
34. Q: What is the alignment problem in artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
The alignment problem concerns how to ensure that an AI system's behavior remains consistent with the objectives, values, constraints, and legitimate interests it is intended to serve. A system may satisfy a narrow instruction while violating the broader purpose behind it. For example, an assistant optimized solely for completing requests might produce an answer that is fast and persuasive but unsafe, misleading, or contrary to organizational policy. Alignment is therefore broader than improving factual accuracy or teaching polite communication. It involves defining acceptable behavior, evaluating difficult cases, preventing foreseeable harm, limiting inappropriate actions, and monitoring the system after deployment.
Study instruction:
- Define the intended behavior of an AI assistant for a workplace task, including prohibited actions, escalation rules, user authority, and measurable success criteria.
Key point:
- Alignment connects model behavior with intended objectives and constraints.
- A useful system must be evaluated for both successful task completion and harmful outcomes.
Source links:
- Generative AI Risk Management Profile — NIST
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
35. Q: How should an organization choose which tasks to delegate to AI and which to retain under direct human control?
A: Show answer
Start by decomposing the workflow into tasks and estimating their complexity, repeatability, consequences of failure, data sensitivity, and verification cost. AI may be suitable for drafting, classification, summarization, brainstorming, and other activities where errors can be detected before use. Tasks involving legal obligations, significant financial commitments, personnel decisions, safety, or sensitive information may require stronger restrictions or direct human decision-making. Some processes can use AI for preparation while reserving approval and execution for authorized personnel. The appropriate boundary depends on the use case, the model's demonstrated reliability, applicable law, and organizational policy.
Study instruction:
- Map one workflow into AI-assisted steps, human review steps, approval gates, and activities that must remain restricted.
Key point:
- Delegation should reflect both model capability and the consequences of failure.
- Human accountability and authorization must remain clear.
Source links:
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST

36. Q: What risks arise when AI is used for consequential or high-stakes decisions?
A: Show answer
Errors in consequential settings can affect finances, employment, health, legal rights, safety, or access to services. Risks include fabricated information, missed context, discriminatory outcomes, privacy exposure, unreliable recommendations, overreliance by users, and weak accountability. A system that performs adequately in routine cases may fail on unusual circumstances or on individuals poorly represented in its evaluation data. Controls may include limiting the system's authority, requiring qualified human review, providing users with a way to challenge decisions, testing across relevant groups, and monitoring incidents. Some applications may be unsuitable for AI assistance even after safeguards are considered.
Study instruction:
- Develop a risk assessment for an AI-supported decision process. Document foreseeable harms, likelihood, severity, mitigation measures, and criteria for suspending use.
Key point:
- The required level of oversight rises with the consequences of error.
- Human involvement must provide meaningful review rather than automatic approval.

37. Q: How should an organization protect confidential information when using generative AI?
A: Show answer
Organizations should identify what information users may submit, which approved systems can process it, how information is retained, and who is authorized to access results. Sensitive personal information, confidential contracts, credentials, unpublished financial data, and protected organizational records require particular care. Users should not assume that every AI service offers identical data-handling terms or security controls. Approved deployment settings, access permissions, data minimization, retention policies, and staff training should be matched to the sensitivity of the information. Output sharing also needs review because the response may repeat or expose sensitive details supplied in the prompt.
Study instruction:
- Classify typical workplace information by sensitivity and define which AI environments and review controls are permitted for each category.
Key point:
- Data handling depends on the specific service, settings, agreements, and organizational requirements.
- Only necessary information should be shared through approved systems.

38. Q: How can an AI-assisted workflow be designed so that errors are detected before they cause harm?
A: Show answer
A robust workflow combines input validation, clearly defined task boundaries, controlled access, output checks, escalation rules, and documented approval. For example, an AI system may draft a financial reconciliation explanation from supplied records, but the reconciliation totals should be recalculated independently and unresolved discrepancies should be presented to an authorized reviewer. Outputs should be traceable to their source material, and changes should be recorded where necessary. Testing should include missing inputs, contradictory evidence, unusually large values, misleading instructions, and cases that should trigger refusal or escalation. Monitoring should identify repeated failures so that procedures and training can be improved.
Study instruction:
- Design a workflow with preventive, detective, and corrective controls. Specify who acts when a check fails and what evidence demonstrates resolution.
Key point:
- Reliable AI-assisted work requires controls throughout the process, not just a final glance at the answer.
- Exceptions should be visible, assigned, escalated, and resolved.

39. Q: How can the effectiveness of an AI-assisted process be measured after deployment?
A: Show answer
Establish a baseline before introducing AI and select metrics that correspond to the intended outcome. Relevant measures may include task completion time, error rate, rework, consistency, user satisfaction, operating cost, and the proportion of cases requiring escalation. Include measures of harm or failure rather than measuring speed alone. Compare outputs with a dependable reference standard and analyze performance across task categories and relevant user groups. Review whether time savings are offset by verification costs or additional corrections. Deployment should remain subject to periodic reassessment because data, user behavior, model versions, and operating conditions can change.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a pilot evaluation plan containing baseline measurements, success thresholds, error categories, review dates, and conditions for expansion or withdrawal.
Key point:
- Productivity gains must be measured alongside accuracy and risk.
- Continued monitoring is necessary to confirm that a deployed system remains suitable.

40. Q: What should managers consider before scaling an AI pilot into an organization-wide service?
A: Show answer
Managers should confirm that the pilot solves a genuine problem, performs reliably on representative tasks, and produces benefits that justify its total cost. They should also assess data availability, security, legal requirements, integration with existing systems, vendor dependence, accessibility, and the workload created by human review. The operating model needs a named service owner, clear user permissions, training, support arrangements, incident reporting, and a process for updating or replacing the system. Scaling should proceed in stages with explicit checkpoints. A successful pilot does not eliminate the need for further testing because larger deployments introduce additional users, data sources, edge cases, and operational dependencies.
Study instruction:
- Draft a go-or-no-go decision paper for a pilot. Include benefits, unresolved risks, expected operating costs, governance responsibilities, and expansion criteria.
Key point:
- Successful experimentation does not automatically justify unrestricted deployment.
- Scaling requires governance, support, continued evaluation, and clear accountability.

Table 2
AI Alignment, Ethics, and Governance
1. Q: What does AI alignment mean, and why is it important?
A: Show answer
AI alignment is the challenge of designing, developing, and operating AI systems so that their behavior supports legitimate human objectives and respects human interests. A system can perform a task exceptionally well without understanding whether pursuing that task creates unacceptable consequences. Alignment therefore involves more than accuracy or technical capability. It also concerns safety, fairness, privacy, controllability, accountability, and the effects of AI on individuals and society. Alignment decisions occur throughout the AI lifecycle, including training, testing, deployment, monitoring, and modification. The appropriate safeguards depend on the system's capabilities, intended use, potential consequences, and the people affected. Alignment should be treated as a continuing responsibility rather than a one-time technical adjustment.
Study instruction:
- Explain how technical performance differs from alignment with human interests, and identify three safeguards appropriate to a high-impact AI application.
Key point:
- A capable AI system is not necessarily a safe or well-aligned system.
- Alignment requires technical safeguards, human accountability, and ongoing evaluation.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
2. Q: What does the paperclip-maximizer thought experiment teach us about AI alignment?
A: Show answer
The paperclip-maximizer thought experiment illustrates the danger of giving a highly capable system a narrowly specified objective without adequately accounting for its effects on people and the environment. A hypothetical system instructed to maximize paperclip production might pursue that objective in ways that consume resources, resist interruption, or disregard human welfare. The important lesson is not that an actual AI will inevitably behave this way. It is that successful task execution does not automatically produce appropriate judgment about consequences. A system needs constraints, contextual understanding, appropriate permissions, and mechanisms that allow people to intervene. Objectives should be designed and evaluated against broader requirements instead of assuming that maximizing one measurable outcome will produce the desired overall result.
Study instruction:
- Construct a paperclip-maximizer example and identify the narrow objective, unintended consequences, missing constraints, and corrective safeguards.
Key point:
- Optimizing a single metric can create outcomes that conflict with broader human interests.
- Objectives must be assessed alongside constraints, consequences, and oversight.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
3. Q: Why might an AI system seek additional resources or resist being shut down even when nobody explicitly instructs it to do so?
A: Show answer
A system pursuing a persistent objective may identify intermediate actions that improve its ability to achieve that objective. Depending on its design and operating environment, access to resources, information, computing capacity, or continued operation might appear useful for completing the assigned task. Such behavior is a potential concern when a system can plan, use tools, or take actions with limited supervision. It does not imply that every AI system possesses self-preservation instincts or independently develops intentions. The practical concern is whether the system's objective, capabilities, and permissions permit undesirable strategies. Developers and deployers should test for unwanted goal-directed behavior, restrict consequential actions, and ensure that authorized humans can interrupt or revoke access when necessary.
Study instruction:
- Analyze an autonomous scheduling or purchasing agent and identify which permissions could allow it to exceed its intended responsibilities.
Key point:
- Unexpected strategies may arise from the interaction between objectives and capabilities.
- Bounded permissions and reliable interruption mechanisms reduce operational risk.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework: Generative AI Profile
4. Q: What is the difference between artificial general intelligence (AGI) and artificial superintelligence (ASI)?
A: Show answer
Artificial general intelligence is a proposed form of AI capable of performing a broad range of intellectual tasks at a level comparable to human capability. Artificial superintelligence describes a hypothetical system that substantially exceeds human intellectual performance across important domains. These concepts represent different levels or scopes of capability, not established classifications that can be applied uniformly to every present-day model. There is no settled certainty about whether AGI or ASI will emerge, what form they might take, or when they might become possible. Planning should therefore distinguish demonstrated capabilities from forecasts. Organizations can manage current AI risks without assuming that superintelligence is imminent, while researchers and policymakers can investigate longer-term risks without treating speculative scenarios as proven outcomes.
Study instruction:
- Compare current task-specific or general-purpose AI capabilities with the hypothetical characteristics attributed to AGI and ASI.
Key point:
- AGI and ASI describe hypothetical capability levels, not guaranteed stages of technological development.
- Risk decisions should distinguish current evidence from uncertain forecasts.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
5. Q: Why does AI alignment include both long-term existential risks and immediate ethical problems?
A: Show answer
Long-term alignment discussions examine hypothetical advanced systems that could create large-scale or irreversible harm. Immediate alignment problems concern risks already relevant to deployed technologies, including discriminatory outputs, privacy violations, deceptive content, unsafe recommendations, manipulated behavior, and cybersecurity weaknesses. These categories differ in their evidence base, likelihood, scale, and required interventions, but both concern whether AI behavior produces acceptable outcomes. Focusing exclusively on distant scenarios can distract organizations from current harms. Conversely, resolving immediate problems would not prove that more advanced systems are safe. A comprehensive approach allocates appropriate attention to present-day operational issues and uncertain future risks, using proportionate controls and revisiting priorities as evidence changes.
Study instruction:
- Classify five AI risks as immediate operational, broader societal, or longer-term hypothetical risks, and explain why the categories may overlap.
Key point:
- Present-day harms and speculative existential risks require distinct but compatible responses.
- Risk management should be proportional to evidence, severity, and the potential for irreversible harm.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
6. Q: Why is it difficult to translate human values into instructions that an AI system can follow reliably?
A: Show answer
Human values are complex, context-dependent, and sometimes contradictory. People may value safety and autonomy, privacy and convenience, equality and individual choice, or transparency and confidentiality simultaneously. The appropriate decision depends on circumstances, affected stakeholders, applicable rights, and competing obligations. A short instruction or numerical reward cannot necessarily represent every relevant consideration. Developers must decide which preferences to encode, how to resolve conflicts, and when a system should defer to a person. Those decisions can introduce unintended assumptions or unfair trade-offs. Reliable alignment therefore requires explicit objectives, clearly defined constraints, diverse evaluation, and procedures for handling ambiguity. Where a decision has material consequences, the system should not be expected to invent an authoritative ethical resolution without appropriate human and institutional guidance.
Study instruction:
- Choose a healthcare, employment, or education scenario and identify two values that may conflict, then propose a defensible decision process.
Key point:
- Human values cannot always be reduced to one simple objective or reward function.
- Ambiguous or conflicting values require context-sensitive safeguards and accountable judgment.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
7. Q: Why should uncertainty about superintelligence not prevent people from addressing current AI problems?
A: Show answer
Uncertainty about advanced AI does not remove the need to make practical decisions about technologies already affecting work, education, research, public services, and communication. Organizations must decide what information they permit AI systems to access, which tasks they delegate, how they assess outputs, and who is accountable for mistakes. These choices affect outcomes regardless of whether artificial general intelligence eventually emerges. Waiting for every scientific or philosophical disagreement to be resolved could leave important present-day risks unmanaged. At the same time, current practical concerns should not be used to dismiss legitimate research into future capabilities. A productive approach acknowledges uncertainty, uses evidence to prioritize action, and preserves opportunities to revise decisions when the technology or risk assessment changes.
Study instruction:
- Write an AI policy addressing three current operational risks and one longer-term uncertainty without making unsupported predictions.
Key point:
- Important AI governance decisions are required before every question about future AI is resolved.
- Evidence-based action and continued research can proceed together.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
8. Q: How does the data used to train AI influence its behavior and ethical characteristics?
A: Show answer
Training data exposes an AI model to patterns in language, images, decisions, and other recorded material. Those patterns influence the model's outputs and can transmit stereotypes, omissions, misleading associations, and assumptions present in the underlying data. The effects depend on the model architecture, training process, data selection, subsequent adjustments, and how the system is used. A large dataset is not necessarily a representative dataset, and including more material does not automatically eliminate harmful patterns. Developers should examine data provenance, quality, composition, limitations, and lawful use. Deployers should also test the resulting model in the intended context because shortcomings may only become visible in particular languages, communities, or applications.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a dataset with uneven representation could influence an AI hiring assistant and propose tests to detect resulting disparities.
Key point:
- Training data influences model behavior but does not determine every output in a simple one-to-one manner.
- Data quality, coverage, provenance, and contextual testing all matter.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
9. Q: Why must organizations distinguish between the legal permission to use training data and the ethical justification for using it?
A: Show answer
Legal permission and ethical justification answer related but different questions. A proposed use of information may be legally permitted in a particular jurisdiction while still raising concerns about consent, privacy, compensation, attribution, representation, or the effects on people whose work contributed to training. Conversely, an ethical argument alone does not establish that a use complies with copyright, privacy, contract, or other applicable laws. An assessment should document the origin of data, applicable rights, permitted uses, restrictions, potential harm, and available alternatives. Where third-party content is involved, organizations should obtain appropriate legal advice rather than assume that publicly accessible material is automatically unrestricted. Ethical review should consider affected groups and possible consequences in addition to minimum legal requirements.
Study instruction:
- Create a decision checklist that evaluates data provenance, legal permissions, consent expectations, privacy, and potential harm separately.
Key point:
- Lawfulness and ethical acceptability are not interchangeable judgments.
- Data governance should account for both applicable rights and foreseeable social consequences.
Source links:
- U.S. Copyright Office: Copyright and Artificial Intelligence
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
10. Q: Why do copyright and AI-training rules need to be evaluated separately across jurisdictions?
A: Show answer
Countries differ in their copyright legislation, exceptions, privacy requirements, enforcement mechanisms, and approaches to computational analysis of protected material. The legal implications of training on copyrighted works therefore cannot be reduced to one universal rule. The relevant analysis may depend on the jurisdiction, the nature and source of the material, the purpose of use, contractual restrictions, and the specific activity performed. The United States Copyright Office has examined copyrightability and the use of copyrighted material in generative AI training, illustrating that these are distinct legal questions. An organization operating internationally should document which laws apply and seek jurisdiction-specific advice. It should not infer that a practice is lawful everywhere merely because it is permitted, tolerated, or unresolved somewhere else.
Study instruction:
- Compare the applicable legal questions for AI training in two jurisdictions and identify the issues that require qualified legal advice.
Key point:
- Copyright and data-protection rules vary by jurisdiction and may develop over time.
- Public availability alone does not establish unrestricted permission to use material for training.
Source links:
- U.S. Copyright Office: Copyright and Artificial Intelligence
- European Union AI Act Explorer
11. Q: Does training an AI model on a work mean that the model will directly copy that work?
A: Show answer
Training does not mean that every item in the dataset is stored and reproduced as an ordinary, retrievable document. A model typically learns statistical patterns represented through its parameters, and many generated outputs are new combinations rather than literal copies of particular training examples. However, this does not establish that memorization never occurs. Models can reproduce portions of training material under some circumstances, especially where examples are unusually repetitive or distinctive. Similarity can also raise questions about protected expression, attribution, and the rights of creators. Technical behavior and legal conclusions should be assessed separately. Developers should test for unintended memorization, while organizations should evaluate generated material before publication or commercial use where copyright or confidentiality risks matter.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish learned patterns, paraphrasing, memorization, and reproduction of protected expression, then identify a test that could reveal excessive memorization.
Key point:
- Generative models do not merely function as databases, but they can still reproduce memorized material.
- Novel generation does not automatically resolve copyright or confidentiality concerns.
Source links:
- U.S. Copyright Office: Copyright and Artificial Intelligence
12. Q: How can unrepresentative training data create biased AI outputs?
A: Show answer
A dataset may overrepresent certain countries, languages, demographic groups, professions, or online communities while omitting others. Models trained on such material can learn associations that inaccurately reflect real populations. Historical prejudice, stereotypes in published material, uneven data collection, and decisions about which sources to include can contribute to the problem. Bias may affect the language an AI uses, the people it depicts, the recommendations it makes, or the confidence with which it presents an unsupported generalization. The problem is not limited to explicit discriminatory instructions: statistical patterns may encode imbalances indirectly. Identifying bias therefore requires examining the training and evaluation data, testing outputs across relevant groups, and investigating whether observed differences create meaningful harm in the intended application.
Study instruction:
- Identify three potential sources of representational bias in a multilingual dataset and explain how each could influence an AI application's results.
Key point:
- Imbalanced data can produce systematic distortions in model outputs.
- Bias assessment should consider both data composition and practical consequences.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
13. Q: What practical steps help determine whether an AI training dataset adequately represents its intended population?
A: Show answer
Start by defining the population, languages, settings, and decisions the model is expected to support. Examine the data's sources, time period, collection methods, demographic coverage, missing categories, and known limitations. Then compare dataset composition with credible information about the intended population and test whether important groups are consistently represented. For generated content, evaluate whether prompts describing comparable people or situations produce systematically different responses or portrayals. Documentation should explain why certain data was included or excluded and what uncertainties remain. Perfect representation is rarely achievable, but explicit limitations allow reviewers to judge where a model is reliable and where additional evidence, narrower deployment, or human review is necessary.
Study instruction:
- Draft a dataset card for a hypothetical public-service AI system, identifying its population, sources, coverage gaps, intended use, and limitations.
Key point:
- Representativeness must be assessed relative to the intended population and task.
- Dataset documentation makes limitations visible to developers, reviewers, and users.

- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
14. Q: How should organizations test image-generating AI for racial, gender, and occupational stereotypes?
A: Show answer
Organizations should define test prompts that cover relevant occupations, social roles, demographic descriptions, and realistic settings. They should generate sufficiently varied samples, document the prompt design, and assess outcomes using transparent criteria. Evaluation can examine representation, stereotyped associations, differences between comparable groups, and whether the images accurately reflect the task's context. A single striking result is not enough to establish a general pattern; repeated and systematically designed tests provide more informative evidence. Quantitative measures should be accompanied by qualitative review because simple demographic counts may not reveal harmful contextual assumptions. Where a system is intended for advertising, education, employment, or public communication, reviewers should assess how these patterns could affect the people represented and the audiences consuming the images.
Study instruction:
- Design an image-generation test matrix for three occupations and multiple demographic groups, then define the evidence required before drawing conclusions about bias.
Key point:
- Controlled, repeated testing is more informative than isolated examples.
- Evaluation should consider both representation and the context in which images will be used.
