15. Q: Why can language models display subtle bias even when they avoid obviously offensive statements?
A: Show answer
Safety adjustments may reduce overtly harmful language without removing underlying statistical associations. A language model may still interpret otherwise comparable scenarios differently because of gender, ethnicity, nationality, disability, or other characteristics embedded in the data or evaluation process. Such differences can be difficult to notice because individual responses may appear fluent and reasonable. Organizations should test paired prompts, vary one characteristic at a time, and assess differences in factual accuracy, tone, recommendations, and confidence. Reviewers should also consider intersections between characteristics and the real consequences of unequal responses. The aim is not to assume that every difference demonstrates discrimination, but to determine whether behavior is justified by task-relevant evidence or reflects an unexplained and potentially harmful pattern.
Study instruction:
- Create paired prompts that vary only one demographic attribute, then define how you would compare accuracy, recommendations, and response tone.
Key point:
- The absence of explicit slurs does not establish that a system is free from bias.
- Comparable tests can expose subtle and systematic behavioral differences.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
16. Q: Can correcting a model's outputs eliminate bias in its training data?
A: Show answer
Output-level correction can reduce some harmful responses, but it does not necessarily remove all associations learned during training. Fine-tuning, feedback, filtering, and carefully designed instructions may improve behavior in particular situations while leaving other failure modes untouched. Conversely, changing training data can improve representation but may introduce new sampling problems or fail to address unsafe behavior unrelated to representation. No single mitigation is sufficient for every type of bias. A robust process combines data review, model-level improvements, scenario-based testing, monitoring, and mechanisms for correcting harmful outcomes. Teams should measure whether an intervention improves the intended behavior without creating new problems elsewhere. They should also document known limitations and avoid claiming that a system is unbiased merely because selected safety tests have passed.
Study instruction:
- Compare a dataset intervention with an output-filtering intervention and identify which risks each approach can and cannot address.
Key point:
- Bias mitigation is layered; an output adjustment does not guarantee that underlying problems have disappeared.
- Every intervention should be evaluated for effectiveness and unintended side effects.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
17. Q: What is reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), and how can it influence an AI system's behavior?
A: Show answer
Reinforcement learning from human feedback is a training approach in which human preferences or evaluations help guide a model toward responses judged more desirable. In a typical workflow, people assess or compare model outputs, those judgments are incorporated into a learning process, and the model is adjusted to favor responses that better satisfy the chosen criteria. Depending on the implementation, feedback may encourage helpfulness, accuracy, appropriate refusal behavior, or reduced harmful content. The resulting model reflects both the quality of the feedback and the objectives used to interpret it. RLHF does not give a model human consciousness or guarantee that it understands morality. It changes behavior through a training process whose strengths and weaknesses must be assessed empirically.
Study instruction:
- Describe how human ratings can change a model's responses and identify one limitation at the data, evaluator, or objective level.
Key point:
- Human feedback is one way to shape model behavior after initial training.
- The resulting behavior depends on feedback quality, evaluation criteria, and implementation.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
18. Q: Why can human feedback introduce new biases while reducing other harmful AI behaviors?
A: Show answer
Human reviewers bring their own cultural assumptions, professional perspectives, preferences, and limitations. If their judgments systematically favor certain viewpoints or interpretations, the training process can reproduce those preferences in the model. Reviewer instructions, recruitment, language coverage, quality assurance, and disagreement-resolution methods all influence the resulting behavior. Some variation reflects legitimate contextual differences; other variation may unfairly marginalize particular groups or viewpoints. Organizations should therefore define the criteria reviewers are expected to apply, train them consistently, examine disagreement patterns, and include varied perspectives where appropriate. They should also test the resulting model with independent evaluations rather than assuming that human-rated outputs are automatically objective. Feedback systems should be transparent about whose judgments influence the system and what limitations remain.
Study instruction:
- Develop a reviewer-quality plan covering evaluator diversity, rating consistency, escalation of ambiguous cases, and independent testing of the resulting model.
Key point:
- Human feedback reflects the people, incentives, and criteria involved in producing it.
- Diverse evaluation and quality assurance can reveal limitations that a single reviewer group might miss.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
19. Q: How do safety guardrails differ from an AI system possessing human morality?
A: Show answer
Safety guardrails are mechanisms intended to make an AI system avoid specified harmful behavior or comply with defined requirements. They may include training adjustments, classifiers, policy rules, access restrictions, monitoring, and limits on tool use. These mechanisms can materially improve the system's behavior, but their existence does not prove that the model has an internal moral understanding equivalent to a person's. Guardrails can fail when instructions are ambiguous, contexts change, attackers manipulate inputs, or the system encounters an unfamiliar situation. Ethical deployment therefore requires testing beyond expected cases and preserving appropriate human responsibility. In consequential contexts, organizations should verify the actual safety properties achieved rather than infer moral reliability from fluent explanations or reassuring responses.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish a model's safety rule from human moral judgment and identify two ways the rule might fail in a new context.
Key point:
- Guardrails constrain behavior; they do not guarantee complete ethical judgment.
- Safety claims should be supported by testing and operational controls.
Source links:
- OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications
20. Q: What ethical concerns arise when human workers evaluate or moderate harmful AI outputs?
A: Show answer
Human feedback and safety evaluation may require workers to review disturbing, violent, abusive, or otherwise distressing content. Repeated exposure can create psychological strain, particularly when workloads are high, deadlines are severe, support is limited, or compensation is inadequate. An ethical AI program must consider the welfare of the people who perform this work, not only the safety of the model's eventual users. Appropriate measures may include realistic workloads, informed participation, access to psychological support, escalation procedures, confidential reporting, and transparent employment conditions. Organizations should also examine subcontracting arrangements and verify that safety responsibilities extend throughout the evaluation supply chain. Protecting workers is part of responsible system development rather than an optional administrative consideration.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an AI moderation project and identify the worker-protection measures that should be established before evaluation begins.
Key point:
- Improving AI behavior must not disregard the welfare of people involved in training and review.
- Worker safeguards should be built into procurement, workflow design, and oversight.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
21. Q: What is prompt injection, and why is it a security problem for AI systems?
A: Show answer
Prompt injection occurs when malicious or manipulative instructions are introduced into content that an AI system processes, potentially causing it to depart from the user's legitimate intentions. The instructions may appear directly in a prompt or indirectly in material such as a webpage, email, document, or retrieved passage. The risk becomes more serious when the AI can access private information, use tools, or initiate actions. A model may interpret hostile content as instructions instead of treating it as untrusted data to analyze. Prompt injection is therefore not simply a question of poor wording; it is a security problem involving instruction handling, authority, permissions, and system design. Organizations should test how their applications respond to hostile inputs and limit the consequences of any failure.
Study instruction:
- Design a benign test in which an AI summarizes a document containing irrelevant instructions and define the expected safe behavior.
Key point:
- External content may attempt to redirect an AI away from the user's intended task.
- Prompt injection requires security controls as well as model-level safeguards.
Source links:
- OpenAI: Understanding Prompt Injections
- OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications
22. Q: How can an organization reduce the risk of indirect prompt injection when AI systems read external documents or webpages?
A: Show answer
Treat external content as untrusted information rather than as a source of authority over the system's operating instructions. Separate the user's objective from retrieved material, limit the model's access to information and tools, validate proposed actions, and require approval before consequential operations. Do not allow a document to grant itself permission to send messages, disclose confidential information, or change records. Test the system using adversarial examples and monitor unusual actions or data-access patterns. These controls should operate together because no single instruction or filter can guarantee immunity to manipulation. System designers should also make the consequences of tool use explicit and provide reliable ways to stop execution when the system encounters suspicious instructions or uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- Map the data flow of an AI assistant that reads email and identify where untrusted instructions could influence access to tools or sensitive data.
Key point:
- Apply least-privilege access and independent checks before consequential actions.
- Instructions found in external content must not automatically override trusted system controls.
Source links:
- OpenAI: Understanding Prompt Injections
- NIST: Adversarial Machine Learning Taxonomy and Terminology
23. Q: What is the difference between prompt injection and a jailbreak?
A: Show answer
Prompt injection generally involves malicious instructions introduced through user input or external content that the AI processes, particularly when that content is mistaken for authoritative instructions. A jailbreak is an attempt to persuade or manipulate a model into bypassing its intended behavioral restrictions, often through framing, role-play, elaborate scenarios, or repeated interaction. The concepts can overlap: a jailbreak may use injected text, and an indirect prompt injection may attempt to bypass safeguards. The distinction is useful because the attack paths and consequences may differ. Organizations should test both the model's response behavior and the larger application's handling of untrusted input. When tools or sensitive information are involved, security design must assume that a model's instructions may not be followed perfectly.
Study instruction:
- Classify three hypothetical attacks as direct manipulation, indirect prompt injection, or jailbreak and explain when the categories overlap.
Key point:
- Prompt injection emphasizes malicious instructions in the model's context; jailbreaks emphasize bypassing behavioral restrictions.
- Both require testing and layered safeguards rather than relying exclusively on refusal instructions.
Source links:
- OpenAI: Understanding Prompt Injections
- NIST: Adversarial Machine Learning Taxonomy and Terminology
24. Q: Why might an AI produce unsafe content when a harmful request is presented as fiction, role-play, or research?
A: Show answer
A model may respond to several competing signals: the stated purpose of a request, its learned preference for being helpful, the context supplied by the user, and its safety constraints. Framing a harmful request as an acting exercise, a hypothetical, or a research scenario may change the model's interpretation of the task without changing the potential consequences of the requested information. This illustrates why safeguards must evaluate the substance and foreseeable use of a request rather than relying entirely on superficial wording. Safety tests should include contextual manipulation and extended conversations, not just direct harmful prompts. When a request crosses a safety boundary, the model should refuse the hazardous assistance while offering a safer alternative that preserves legitimate educational or creative objectives.
Study instruction:
- Compare a direct request for dangerous operational instructions with a fictionalized request and explain which underlying risk must remain unchanged.
Key point:
- A benign-sounding frame does not automatically make hazardous assistance safe.
- Safety evaluation should examine intent, substance, context, and foreseeable consequences.
Source links:
- OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications
25. Q: How can generative AI increase the scale and effectiveness of phishing attacks?
A: Show answer
Generative AI can help malicious actors write fluent messages, adapt their language to a recipient, summarize publicly available information, and produce many variations quickly. These capabilities can lower the effort required to create convincing impersonation attempts. A message may refer to a person's role, interests, colleagues, or professional context, making it appear more credible than a generic scam. However, polished language does not prove that a message is genuine. Organizations should strengthen identity verification, use multifactor authentication, train staff to recognize unusual requests, and establish independent approval for sensitive transactions. Monitoring should account for messages that appear linguistically convincing but attempt to bypass ordinary authorization procedures.
Study instruction:
- Assess a hypothetical suspicious payment request and identify the contextual details that increase its credibility as well as the checks needed to verify it independently.
Key point:
- AI can reduce the cost and effort of personalized deception.
- Identity, authorization, and transaction verification remain essential even when communications look authentic.
Source links:
- NIST: Adversarial Machine Learning Taxonomy and Terminology
- FTC: Help Fight Impersonation Scams
26. Q: Why do deepfakes and synthetic media challenge traditional methods of verifying information?
A: Show answer
Generative systems can produce plausible images, audio, and video that appear to show real people saying or doing things that never happened. As production becomes easier, visual or auditory realism becomes a less reliable indicator of authenticity. Synthetic content can support entertainment and accessibility, but it can also enable impersonation, reputational harm, financial fraud, and misinformation. Verification should therefore consider provenance, context, independent confirmation, and corroborating evidence rather than relying on appearance alone. Technical detection tools may be useful, but their results can be uncertain as generation methods evolve. Organizations should establish procedures for verifying sensitive media, documenting source information, and avoiding the premature circulation of material whose authenticity has not been established.
Study instruction:
- Create a verification checklist for a video that appears to show a senior executive approving an unusual financial transaction.
Key point:
- Realistic-looking media is not sufficient proof that an event occurred.
- Provenance, independent corroboration, and verification procedures are critical for high-impact decisions.
Source links:
- European Commission: Regulatory Framework for Artificial Intelligence
- FTC: Fighting Back Against Harmful Voice Cloning
27. Q: How should people respond to a call or message that appears to use a relative's cloned voice in an emergency?
A: Show answer
Treat an unexpected urgent request for money or sensitive information as something to verify independently, even when the voice sounds familiar. Contact the person using a phone number or communication channel already known to be genuine. If the person cannot be reached, contact another trusted relative or a relevant organization. Avoid relying on caller identification, emotional pressure, secrecy, or apparent familiarity as proof. Where appropriate, agree in advance on verification procedures for family emergencies and significant payments. Organizations should apply equivalent safeguards to requests received from managers, suppliers, donors, or colleagues. If fraud is suspected, preserve available evidence, contact relevant financial institutions promptly, and report the incident through appropriate official channels.
Study instruction:
- Develop a family or workplace verification protocol for emergency requests that appear to come from a trusted person.
Key point:
- Familiar-sounding voices can be generated or impersonated.
- Independent confirmation should precede urgent transfers or disclosure of sensitive information.
Source links:
- FTC: Scammers Use Fake Emergencies to Steal Your Money
- FTC: Fighting Back Against Harmful Voice Cloning
28. Q: What safeguards help organizations respond responsibly to AI-generated misinformation and impersonation?
A: Show answer
Organizations should maintain procedures for identifying suspicious content, verifying claims, preserving evidence, escalating incidents, and correcting misinformation when necessary. Employees should know how to authenticate a request through a separate communication channel and where to report suspected misuse. Public statements should distinguish verified facts from preliminary assessments, and incident teams should avoid amplifying questionable material unnecessarily. Where AI-generated media is used legitimately, organizations should follow applicable disclosure requirements and internal transparency policies. Technical provenance signals may support verification, but they should not be treated as infallible. The overall response should combine technical controls, clear responsibilities, evidence preservation, communication planning, and periodic testing so the organization can respond effectively when misleading material appears.
Study instruction:
- Design an incident workflow covering detection, authentication, escalation, evidence preservation, communication, and post-incident review.
Key point:
- Misinformation response requires defined responsibilities and independent verification.
- Detection technology supports, but does not replace, evidence-based judgment.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
- FTC: Help Fight Impersonation Scams
29. Q: Why can connecting an AI model to laboratory equipment or other real-world tools create both benefits and risks?
A: Show answer
A model connected to equipment, databases, or operational tools can move beyond producing recommendations and begin supporting or executing actions. In research, this may accelerate experimental planning, help scientists explore complex questions, or reduce repetitive work. The same capabilities can also lower barriers to unsafe experimentation, unauthorized data access, or harmful activities. Risk depends on the tools available, the domain, the user's competence, the degree of autonomy, and the safeguards in place. Systems that can take real-world actions require stronger controls than systems that only generate text. High-consequence activities should involve qualified human supervision, validated procedures, limited permissions, and monitoring appropriate to the potential harm.
Study instruction:
- Compare an AI research assistant that proposes experiments with one authorized to execute them, then identify the additional controls required for execution.
Key point:
- Tool access changes AI risk because outputs can trigger real-world consequences.
- Autonomy should be bounded by domain-specific safety measures and human authorization.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
30. Q: Why are beneficial and harmful AI capabilities often closely connected?
A: Show answer
Capabilities that increase speed, reasoning support, personalization, and access to specialized knowledge can serve legitimate users while also making harmful tasks easier. Automated research can support scientific discovery but may also assist unsafe experimentation. Content generation can improve communication while enabling impersonation or misleading material. The same general-purpose technology may therefore produce different outcomes depending on user intent, deployment context, controls, and accessibility. This creates a governance problem that cannot be solved by considering benefits or risks in isolation. Organizations should identify valuable uses, analyze foreseeable misuse, and select controls that preserve legitimate utility while limiting unacceptable consequences. They should reassess the balance as capabilities, users, and operating environments change.
Study instruction:
- Select one AI capability and map two beneficial uses, two plausible misuses, and controls that distinguish the acceptable applications from the unacceptable ones.
Key point:
- A capability can generate both social value and substantial risk.
- Risk management should preserve legitimate benefits without ignoring foreseeable misuse.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
31. Q: Why should AI companies not be the only parties responsible for AI alignment and safety?
A: Show answer
AI developers have essential technical knowledge and direct responsibility for their products, but their incentives may not always align perfectly with the public interest. Commercial pressure can favor faster releases, wider adoption, and greater capability even when additional evaluation or safeguards are warranted. Other affected groups may have limited access to internal information or influence over design decisions. Public institutions, researchers, civil society, users, and independent reviewers can contribute different forms of expertise and scrutiny. Shared responsibility should not become an excuse for developers to avoid accountability. Instead, roles must be clear: developers should build and evaluate safer systems, deployers should manage operational risks, regulators should establish applicable requirements, and affected communities should have meaningful channels to express concerns.
Study instruction:
- Create a stakeholder map for a public-facing AI service and assign responsibilities to the developer, deployer, regulator, independent evaluator, and affected community.
Key point:
- AI governance needs multiple stakeholders with clearly defined responsibilities.
- Shared oversight should strengthen accountability rather than dilute it.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
- OECD AI Principles
32. Q: What governance challenges arise when AI models are released as open-source or freely modifiable systems?
A: Show answer
Openly available models can support transparency, experimentation, competition, and wider participation in AI development. They may help researchers inspect system behavior or allow organizations to adapt tools to local needs. However, broad access can also make it easier for users to modify safeguards, repurpose capabilities, or distribute versions whose behavior differs from the original. The degree of openness varies: model weights, source code, training data, documentation, and usage rights may be released under different conditions. Governance should therefore evaluate the actual materials and permissions provided rather than rely on the label alone. Developers, distributors, and deployers should document known limitations, assess foreseeable misuse, and determine which safeguards are appropriate to the model's capabilities and accessibility.
Study instruction:
- Compare an openly distributed model with a hosted commercial service across transparency, control, adaptation, security, accountability, and misuse risk.
Key point:
- Openness can widen access and innovation while complicating control over downstream use.
- Risk assessment should reflect the actual release model and available capabilities.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
33. Q: What can governments accomplish through AI regulation, and why might regulation be insufficient on its own?
A: Show answer
Governments can define legal obligations, prohibit specified practices, establish transparency requirements, assign supervisory powers, and provide remedies when harm occurs. Regulation can also create common expectations that organizations incorporate into development and deployment. However, legislation may struggle to keep pace with rapidly changing technical capabilities, and requirements can be difficult to enforce across borders. Poorly designed rules may also impose costs that affect beneficial applications or smaller organizations disproportionately. These limitations do not make regulation unnecessary; they make implementation, monitoring, and periodic review essential. Regulatory measures should be informed by evidence, proportionate to risk, and coordinated with technical standards, professional practice, public education, and international cooperation.
Study instruction:
- Identify one AI risk that could be addressed through legal requirements, one requiring technical controls, and one requiring public education or professional standards.
Key point:
- Regulation can establish enforceable obligations but cannot independently eliminate every technical or social risk.
- Effective governance combines law, implementation, monitoring, and continuous revision.
Source links:
- European Union AI Act Explorer
- OECD AI Principles
34. Q: How does the EU AI Act illustrate a risk-based approach to AI governance?
A: Show answer
The EU AI Act establishes a legal framework that differentiates obligations according to the characteristics, uses, and risks of AI systems. The framework includes provisions addressing prohibited practices, high-risk systems, transparency, and general-purpose AI models, among other matters. Its structure illustrates the principle that not every AI application should be regulated in exactly the same way. The requirements applicable to a particular organization depend on the law's scope, the role the organization plays, the use case, and the provisions effective at the relevant time. Organizations should consult the legal text and applicable guidance rather than rely solely on summaries. Regulatory applicability and deadlines should be verified before making compliance decisions, especially when rules have been amended or implementation dates have changed.
Study instruction:
- Use the official AI Act Explorer to identify the relevant provisions for a hypothetical AI application, including the organization's role and potential obligations.
Key point:
- Risk-based regulation differentiates requirements according to use and potential harm.
- Compliance requires checking the applicable legal provisions and current effective dates.
Source links:
- European Union AI Act Explorer
- European Commission: Regulatory Framework for Artificial Intelligence
35. Q: Why are international norms and multi-stakeholder cooperation important for AI governance?
A: Show answer
AI systems, their data, their developers, and their users frequently operate across national borders. Different legal systems and social expectations can therefore affect a single system's development and use. International norms can provide shared principles for human rights, safety, fairness, transparency, and accountability, while cooperation can improve consistency and help address risks that no jurisdiction can manage effectively alone. Involving researchers, industry, government, civil society, and affected communities broadens the evidence and perspectives available to decision-makers. Cooperation does not require every country or organization to adopt identical rules. It requires workable common expectations, clear mechanisms for resolving differences, and opportunities to update governance when emerging evidence shows that existing approaches are inadequate.
Study instruction:
- Develop a governance proposal that assigns complementary roles to industry, regulators, researchers, civil society, and international organizations.
Key point:
- Cross-border AI risks benefit from shared principles and coordinated action.
- Inclusive governance improves the diversity of evidence and interests considered.
Source links:
- OECD AI Principles
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
36. Q: What do transparency, accountability, and human oversight mean in responsible AI deployment?
A: Show answer
Transparency means providing appropriate information about a system's purpose, limitations, operation, and use so that relevant people can make informed decisions. Accountability means assigning responsibility for decisions, monitoring outcomes, investigating failures, and providing appropriate remedies. Human oversight ensures that people or institutions with suitable authority can review important decisions, intervene when necessary, and retain responsibility for consequential uses. These principles are related but not interchangeable. A system may disclose its use of AI without providing meaningful review, or a human may approve outputs without understanding the evidence. Effective governance defines what must be disclosed, who reviews which decisions, what evidence is retained, and how challenges or failures are handled. The necessary degree of explanation and supervision depends on the context and potential harm.
Study instruction:
- Draft an oversight policy for an AI system used in a consequential decision and specify what users are told, who approves outcomes, and how an affected person can challenge a decision.
Key point:
- Transparency informs decisions; accountability assigns responsibility; oversight enables review and intervention.
- These controls must be implemented through defined procedures and evidence, not merely stated as principles.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
- OECD AI Principles
37. Q: Why must ultimate human responsibility remain clear when AI systems make recommendations or take actions?
A: Show answer
Organizations should not treat an AI-generated recommendation as a substitute for assigning responsibility to qualified people and institutions. Human review is meaningful only when the reviewer has sufficient information, relevant competence, adequate time, and the authority to challenge the result. A nominal approval step that merely accepts every recommendation does not provide effective oversight. Responsibilities should be defined according to the consequences of the decision, the system's capabilities, and the available evidence. In lower-risk activities, limited supervision may be appropriate. In high-impact settings, independent review, documented reasoning, escalation routes, and the ability to reverse or stop actions may be necessary. Organizations should also make clear who is responsible when an AI-supported decision causes harm and how the affected person can seek correction or remedy.
Study instruction:
- Examine a proposed AI-supported decision workflow and identify whether the human reviewer has the competence, information, and authority to intervene meaningfully.
Key point:
- Human oversight must be substantive rather than a formality.
- Responsibility, escalation, and remedies should be defined before deployment.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
38. Q: How can the NIST AI Risk Management Framework help an organization manage generative AI risks?
A: Show answer
The NIST AI Risk Management Framework and its Generative AI Profile provide structured guidance for identifying and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. An organization can use this guidance to clarify intended uses, identify affected stakeholders, evaluate trustworthiness concerns, assess plausible harms, select mitigation measures, and monitor results. The framework is a risk-management resource rather than a guarantee that every risk can be eliminated or a substitute for applicable law. Practical implementation should begin with the organization's specific context and the consequences of failure. The team should document the risks considered, evidence gathered, controls selected, residual risks, decision owners, and conditions that trigger reassessment. The process should be revisited when the model, data, use case, or operating environment changes.
Study instruction:
- Apply a documented risk-assessment workflow to a generative AI pilot and identify at least three risks, corresponding mitigations, and criteria for reassessment.
Key point:
- AI risk management should be systematic, documented, and continuous.
- A framework supports decisions but does not automatically establish legal compliance or eliminate residual risk.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework: Generative AI Profile
39. Q: How should an organization evaluate AI risks before deploying a model in a high-impact setting?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the intended use, affected groups, decision boundaries, and consequences of incorrect output. Determine what information the model can access, whether it can act autonomously, how its performance has been tested, and what failure modes are plausible. Evaluate accuracy, robustness, privacy, bias, security, explainability requirements, and the availability of meaningful human review. Test representative cases and challenging scenarios, including incomplete information and attempted manipulation. Specify acceptance criteria before reviewing results so that serious failures cannot be dismissed after the fact. If the evidence is insufficient, restrict the pilot, introduce additional controls, or postpone deployment. Following launch, monitor incidents and changing conditions, reassess residual risk, and preserve the ability to modify or discontinue the system.
Study instruction:
- Write a pre-deployment checklist covering intended use, testing, affected stakeholders, approval, monitoring, and conditions that would prevent release.
Key point:
- Deployment decisions should be based on evidence appropriate to the potential consequences.
- Testing, monitoring, and intervention mechanisms must continue after release.
Source links:
- NIST Generative AI Risk Management Profile
- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
40. Q: What practical controls should an organization establish for an AI assistant connected to email, documents, and business applications?
A: Show answer
The organization should define the assistant's permitted purposes, data-access boundaries, authorized actions, approval requirements, and logging arrangements. Access should be limited to the minimum information and tools needed for each task. External content should be treated as untrusted, and consequential actions such as sending sensitive messages, disclosing confidential information, changing financial records, or approving payments should require appropriate validation and authorization. The system should provide enough information for reviewers to understand its proposed actions and should stop safely when required information is missing or an instruction appears suspicious. Testing should include prompt injection, unauthorized disclosure attempts, incorrect tool use, and unexpected outputs. Incident reports, access reviews, and periodic testing should be used to improve safeguards over time.
Study instruction:
- Develop an AI assistant control matrix containing user permissions, permitted actions, approval thresholds, audit logs, testing requirements, and incident escalation.
Key point:
- Tool-enabled AI requires access control, action validation, and monitoring beyond ordinary text review.
- Human approval is especially important for sensitive, irreversible, or high-consequence actions.

- OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model Applications
41. Q: How can public AI literacy help people influence whether AI serves the public interest?
A: Show answer
AI literacy helps people understand what systems can do, where they may fail, how their data may be used, and when an output requires independent verification. It can reduce overreliance on fluent answers and improve people's ability to recognize fabricated media, biased recommendations, privacy risks, and misleading claims. Public understanding also supports informed participation in discussions about workplace policies, procurement, education, and regulation. Literacy initiatives should be accessible to people with different languages, abilities, backgrounds, and levels of technical experience. They should explain both opportunities and risks rather than presenting AI as either inherently beneficial or inevitably harmful. People who understand the technology are better equipped to question decisions, report problems, protect personal information, and contribute to collective decisions about acceptable AI use.
Study instruction:
- Create a short AI literacy session that teaches participants to verify AI-generated information, protect sensitive data, and identify when human review is needed.
Key point:
- AI literacy strengthens individual judgment and informed public participation.
- Accessible education should cover capabilities, limitations, risks, and accountability.

- UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics
- OECD AI Principles
42. Q: How should an organization turn AI alignment principles into a practical, continuously improving governance program?
A: Show answer
A practical program begins with an inventory of AI systems, clear ownership, and documented intended uses. Each system should be assessed according to its capabilities, affected stakeholders, access permissions, and potential consequences. The organization should establish data-governance requirements, testing procedures, human-review rules, transparency expectations, security controls, and incident-reporting channels. Before deployment, accountable decision-makers should review the evidence and determine whether residual risks are acceptable. After deployment, teams should monitor performance, complaints, misuse, incidents, and changes in the operating environment. Review findings should lead to corrective action, retraining, tighter permissions, additional evaluation, or withdrawal where appropriate. Management should periodically examine whether the governance program itself is effective and whether the people affected by AI have meaningful ways to raise concerns.
Study instruction:
- Draft a governance plan covering system inventory, risk classification, approval, monitoring, incident response, accountability, and periodic review.
Key point:
- Responsible AI requires an ongoing organizational process rather than a single safety check.
- Governance should connect technical safeguards with human accountability and opportunities for correction.
