Table 5
AI Creativity, Reliability, and Human Collaboration
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 5, “AI as a Creative”?
A: Show answer
The central argument is that generative AI can expand human creativity by producing ideas, connecting concepts, exploring alternatives, and helping create written, visual, and technical work. However, its creative flexibility is connected to a significant weakness: it can generate plausible but inaccurate information. The practical challenge is therefore not simply deciding whether AI is creative, but learning when to use its generative capabilities, how to evaluate its output, and where human judgment remains essential. The strongest approach treats AI as a contributor to a creative process rather than an unquestionable authority or a complete replacement for human thought.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the relationship between AI's creative potential, its reliability limitations, and the need for human judgment."
Key point:
- AI can broaden creative possibilities while still producing errors.
- Effective collaboration requires both experimentation and critical evaluation.
Source links:
2. Q: What is an AI hallucination, and why can it be difficult to detect?
A: Show answer
An AI hallucination occurs when a generative system produces information that is false, unsupported, internally inconsistent, or otherwise misleading while presenting it as a plausible response. These outputs may contain realistic names, dates, explanations, references, and professional terminology. Their fluency can make them appear trustworthy even when the underlying claims are incorrect. Obvious mistakes may be easy to notice, but subtle factual errors can survive an ordinary review and enter reports, research, presentations, or decisions. Hallucination should therefore be understood as a reliability risk that requires specific verification procedures rather than as an occasional cosmetic flaw.
Study instruction:
- "Identify factual claims in an AI-generated response and distinguish plausible language from independently verified evidence."
Key point:
- Fluency and confidence do not establish factual accuracy.
- Unsupported details require verification before consequential use.
Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
3. Q: How does next-token prediction help explain both AI capabilities and its limitations?
A: Show answer
Large language models generate text by estimating which token is likely to follow the preceding context. During generation, the model repeatedly selects or samples possible continuations until it produces a response. Learning statistical patterns across large datasets enables the system to construct coherent explanations, follow instructions, and combine concepts in useful ways. However, predicting a plausible continuation is not equivalent to retrieving a verified fact or applying a reliable database lookup. A response may consequently sound reasonable without being supported by evidence. This mechanism helps explain why the same technology can produce imaginative combinations and misleading factual claims.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the difference between generating a likely textual continuation and verifying a statement against an authoritative source."
Key point:
- Statistical language generation can support both useful synthesis and factual error.
- Verification requires evidence beyond the plausibility of the generated response.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile, Section 2.2: Confabulation
4. Q: How can training data contribute to unreliable AI answers?
A: Show answer
A model learns patterns from the information used to train and refine it. If that information contains errors, outdated claims, biases, stereotypes, or conflicting interpretations, those weaknesses can influence the model's output. A system may also reproduce associations that are common in its training material without establishing whether those associations are accurate or fair. This means that an answer can be problematic even when the model follows its learned patterns consistently. Users should consider the source, date, completeness, and relevance of information, particularly when the task concerns people, sensitive decisions, specialized knowledge, or changing circumstances.
Study instruction:
- "Assess how biased, incomplete, outdated, or contradictory source material could affect an AI-generated recommendation."
Key point:
- Model output can reflect weaknesses in its training and information sources.
- Reliable use requires attention to evidence quality, context, and bias.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
5. Q: Why are AI-generated citations, quotations, and precise historical details especially risky?
A: Show answer
Specific references require a high degree of factual precision. A model may generate a plausible author, publication title, quotation, case name, page number, or date without having retrieved that information from a dependable source. Familiar material may be reproduced accurately because its patterns are strongly represented in training data, while obscure details may be completed incorrectly. Even a citation that looks professionally formatted can point to a nonexistent work or misrepresent a real one. The appropriate control is to locate the original source, confirm that it exists, and verify that it actually supports the statement being cited.
Study instruction:
- "Select five citations in an AI-generated research note and verify their existence, bibliographic details, and relevance to the associated claims."
Key point:
- A well-formatted citation is not proof of a real source.
- Verify the original source rather than relying on the generated reference.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
6. Q: Why should users not rely on an AI system to explain exactly why it made a mistake?
A: Show answer
An AI-generated explanation of its own response is another generated response. It may offer a convincing account of an error without establishing the actual computational process that produced it. Asking the model why it hallucinated can help identify possible weaknesses, but the explanation should be treated as a hypothesis rather than a definitive record of internal reasoning. A better investigation examines the original prompt, the information supplied, the model's response, external evidence, and the sequence of corrections. Where accuracy matters, conclusions should be based on observable evidence and reproducible checks rather than the model's self-description.
Study instruction:
- "Investigate an incorrect AI response using the original prompt and external evidence, then compare your findings with the model's explanation."
Key point:
- Self-explanations are not independent verification.
- Error analysis should rely on evidence, testing, and documented corrections.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
7. Q: How should organizations decide when AI-generated information requires additional verification?
A: Show answer
Verification should be proportionate to the consequences of an error. An informal brainstorming exercise may tolerate inaccurate or unusual suggestions because the purpose is to explore possibilities. A financial statement, legal submission, medical recommendation, regulatory filing, or safety procedure requires much stricter controls. Organizations should identify the claims that materially affect a decision, determine which sources are authoritative, assign responsibility for checking them, and retain evidence of the review. AI may help organize or explain information, but the final level of assurance must reflect the risks of the task and the standards governing the decision.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five AI-assisted tasks by their consequences of error and specify the evidence and approval each requires."
Key point:
- Verification effort should increase with the potential impact of an error.
- High-consequence decisions need accountable human review and authoritative evidence.
Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative AI Profile
8. Q: Why can the same feature that makes AI unreliable also make it creatively useful?
A: Show answer
Creative work often requires moving beyond familiar combinations and exploring connections that are not immediately obvious. Generative AI can produce unexpected associations because its output is not restricted to retrieving a single established answer. The same flexibility can produce false facts when the task demands precision. The practical distinction lies in how the output is used: an unusual association may be valuable as an idea, while an invented citation is unacceptable as evidence. Users should therefore separate the exploratory stage, where novelty matters, from the verification stage, where accuracy and support matter.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an unexpected idea that stimulates a project with an unsupported factual claim and explain how each should be evaluated."
Key point:
- Novelty can be useful even when the original suggestion is imperfect.
- Creative exploration and factual verification require different evaluation criteria.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
9. Q: What is recombination, and why is it important to understanding AI creativity?
A: Show answer
Recombination involves bringing existing concepts, methods, experiences, or observations together in a new arrangement. Many innovations emerge not from creating every component from nothing, but from recognizing a productive relationship between components that were previously considered separately. AI can help this process by combining concepts across different domains and suggesting alternatives that a person might not immediately consider. However, a novel combination is only a starting point: it may be impractical, derivative, incoherent, or already familiar to others. Human evaluation determines whether the combination solves a meaningful problem and merits further development.
Study instruction:
- "Choose three concepts from unrelated fields, generate possible combinations, and evaluate which combinations could solve a real problem."
Key point:
- Creative novelty often emerges from recombining existing knowledge.
- A surprising combination becomes useful only when its value is assessed.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — The Current State of AI Generative Language Models and Divergent Thinking
10. Q: What does the Alternative Uses Test reveal about generative AI?
A: Show answer
The Alternative Uses Test asks participants to suggest different uses for an ordinary object, such as a paper clip or toothbrush. It is commonly used to examine divergent thinking: the ability to explore multiple possibilities rather than settle immediately on a conventional response. AI can produce long lists of possible uses quickly, making it useful for expanding the initial pool of ideas. Nevertheless, the number of suggestions does not establish that every suggestion is original, safe, practical, or valuable. A meaningful evaluation should consider variety, novelty, feasibility, usefulness, and whether the results advance the specific objective.
Study instruction:
- "Run an Alternative Uses Test with an ordinary object and compare the responses by quantity, diversity, originality, and practicality."
Key point:
- AI can rapidly expand the range of possible ideas.
- Idea count alone is not a complete measure of creativity.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — AI and Divergent Thinking
11. Q: How does the Remote Associates Test illustrate the value of making unexpected connections?
A: Show answer
The Remote Associates Test presents several apparently unrelated words and asks the participant to find a connecting concept. Solving such problems requires identifying a relationship that is not necessarily obvious from any individual word. Language models may perform well on these tasks because they are designed to recognize and generate associations among concepts. However, performance on a controlled word-association test should not be confused with the ability to create an original product, develop a compelling artistic work, or implement an innovation successfully. Such tests measure particular aspects of creative thinking rather than creativity in its entirety.
Study instruction:
- "Complete several word-association exercises and identify which skills they measure and which aspects of real-world creativity they do not test."
Key point:
- Associative ability can support creative problem-solving.
- Test performance does not establish complete real-world creative competence.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — AI and Divergent Thinking
12. Q: What are the limitations of using psychological creativity tests to compare humans with AI?
A: Show answer
Creativity tests examine defined tasks under particular conditions. Results can depend on the instructions, scoring system, number of attempts, model version, and participants' backgrounds. An AI system may generate more responses than a human in the same period, but a longer list can contain repetition or weak suggestions. A system can also perform well on an established test without demonstrating comparable judgment, originality, cultural understanding, or long-term project execution. Research findings should therefore be interpreted according to what the test actually measures, rather than generalized into claims that AI is universally more or less creative than people.
Study instruction:
- "Review a creativity study and identify its sample, test design, scoring criteria, limitations, and the claims that its results can reasonably support."
Key point:
- Creativity is multidimensional and cannot be reduced to one score.
- Conclusions should remain within the limits of the research design.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — Divergent Creativity in Humans and Large Language Models
13. Q: How should fluency, diversity, originality, and usefulness be distinguished when evaluating AI-generated ideas?
A: Show answer
Fluency describes how many ideas are generated. Diversity concerns how meaningfully different those ideas are. Originality concerns their novelty relative to a relevant reference group or existing body of work. Usefulness concerns whether an idea addresses the problem, can be implemented, and creates value. These dimensions are related but not interchangeable. A model may produce dozens of suggestions that differ only in wording, or one highly original proposal that is impractical. A balanced assessment establishes the objective first and then uses suitable criteria to compare candidate ideas.
Study instruction:
- "Score a sample of generated ideas against four separate criteria: fluency, diversity, originality, and usefulness."
Key point:
- More output does not automatically mean more innovation.
- Separate criteria reveal strengths and weaknesses that a single score can hide.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — Divergent Creativity in Humans and Large Language Models
14. Q: What can experiments comparing AI-generated product ideas with human ideas tell us about innovation?
A: Show answer
Such experiments can test whether AI contributes useful candidates under controlled conditions. Chapter 5 discusses an innovation exercise in which AI-generated product proposals performed strongly when evaluated by human judges for their potential appeal. The broader lesson is that generative AI can challenge assumptions about which parts of innovation are difficult to automate. However, judge preferences are only one indicator of commercial potential. Real products must also satisfy customer needs, technical constraints, cost requirements, legal obligations, and implementation conditions. Evaluation should therefore progress from idea generation to customer validation and practical testing.
Study instruction:
- "Take five AI-generated product ideas and evaluate them against customer demand, cost, feasibility, differentiation, and implementation risk."
Key point:
- AI can contribute commercially interesting ideas that deserve investigation.
- Human evaluation and real-world evidence are still needed before implementation.
Source links:
- Thinking Skills and Creativity — Large Language Models and Human Creative Performance
15. Q: Why can AI generate many ideas while still producing limited diversity?
A: Show answer
Generative systems can produce a large volume of suggestions in a short period, but repeated prompts may lead to similar themes, familiar formats, and predictable solutions. Rewording one concept several times increases apparent output without necessarily expanding the space of possibilities. To improve diversity, users can request distinct strategic approaches, vary the constraints, introduce unfamiliar perspectives, and ask the model to identify similarities between its proposals. They can also combine independent human brainstorming with multiple AI-generated batches before selecting a direction.
Study instruction:
- "Generate ideas under three different sets of constraints and compare how the underlying concepts change, not merely how the wording changes."
Key point:
- Output volume and conceptual diversity are different properties.
- Deliberate variation can reduce repetitive brainstorming results.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — Divergent Creativity in Humans and Large Language Models
16. Q: Why should AI brainstorming complement rather than replace human idea generation?
A: Show answer
AI can lower the effort required to produce a broad initial list, which is particularly useful when participants struggle to begin. Human contributors, however, bring personal experience, contextual knowledge, emotional understanding, stakeholder awareness, and their own distinctive associations. The combination can improve exploration when people use AI suggestions as material for further thinking rather than treating the first batch as the answer. Beginning with independent human ideas can also help protect the group from becoming anchored on the model's initial direction. The purpose is to expand the joint search space while retaining human initiative and judgment.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an independent brainstorming session with a session that combines initial human ideas and subsequent AI suggestions."
Key point:
- AI expands the pool of possibilities available to a team.
- Human initiative remains important for diversity, context, and judgment.
Source links:
- Thinking Skills and Creativity — Large Language Models and Collective Creativity
17. Q: How should a team structure an AI-assisted brainstorming session?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the problem, intended audience, constraints, and criteria for a successful outcome. Invite participants to generate initial ideas independently before asking AI to produce alternatives. Organize the combined ideas into distinct categories, identify repetitions, and investigate proposals that offer genuinely different approaches. Ask the AI to critique selected concepts, identify assumptions, or suggest ways to test them, but retain human responsibility for prioritization. Finish with an action list that records the selected proposals, reasons for selection, unresolved questions, and experiments required to determine feasibility.
Study instruction:
- "Facilitate a short brainstorming exercise using independent ideation, AI expansion, clustering, critical evaluation, and a final experiment plan."
Key point:
- A structured process turns generated suggestions into actionable options.
- Selection, validation, and implementation remain explicit responsibilities.
Source links:
- Thinking Skills and Creativity — Human and AI Creative Performance
18. Q: How can assigning AI a professional role improve creative output?
A: Show answer
Describing a role provides the model with useful context about the perspective, vocabulary, and priorities expected in its response. Asking it to act as a marketing strategist, product designer, editor, or researcher can help direct the output toward a particular purpose. However, a role instruction does not give the model genuine professional qualifications or guarantee the accuracy of its advice. Users should combine the role with a clear objective, intended audience, constraints, evaluation criteria, and relevant background information. For specialized work, the result should still be reviewed by someone who understands the discipline.
Study instruction:
- "Run the same creative task with and without a defined professional role and compare relevance, specificity, and quality."
Key point:
- Roles can focus the response on a useful professional perspective.
- A simulated role does not replace genuine expertise or verification.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Risk Management Profile
19. Q: How can prompts be designed to produce genuinely different creative alternatives?
A: Show answer
A useful prompt specifies the objective and the kind of difference required. For example, a campaign concept could be requested through a cost-saving approach, a premium-positioning approach, a community-centered approach, and an unconventional experimental approach. The prompt can also prohibit repeated themes and require a brief explanation of each option's distinctive advantage. After generating the candidates, ask the system to group similar proposals and identify what makes the remaining alternatives different. This method encourages exploration across approaches instead of generating a long list of superficial variations on one idea.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a repetitive brainstorming prompt so that it requires different strategic approaches, explicit constraints, and a comparison of the resulting ideas."
Key point:
- Define diversity in the prompt rather than assuming it will happen automatically.
- Compare concepts by their underlying approach, not just their wording.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — Divergent Creativity in Humans and Large Language Models
20. Q: How should AI be used to select and refine ideas instead of simply generating more of them?
A: Show answer
After generating alternatives, the next task is to distinguish promising candidates from weak or repetitive ones. AI can help develop a comparison matrix, identify missing information, expose assumptions, and propose inexpensive ways to test an idea. The evaluation criteria should be established by the project team rather than invented to justify the model's preferred proposal. Independent evidence, customer feedback, expert judgment, and practical constraints should determine the final decision. A useful workflow therefore alternates generation with critique, testing, and refinement instead of treating the largest list as the best result.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate ten generated proposals against predetermined criteria and create a shortlist with evidence-based reasons for selection."
Key point:
- Creative value emerges through selection and refinement, not generation alone.
- Evaluation criteria should be set before comparing the alternatives.
Source links:
- Thinking Skills and Creativity — AI and Collective Creativity
21. Q: How can AI help overcome a creative block or the difficulty of starting from a blank page?
A: Show answer
A blank page can make it difficult to begin because the person must simultaneously define the problem, select an approach, and produce an initial draft. AI can reduce this initial burden by offering outlines, alternative openings, preliminary concepts, or a list of questions to explore. The important decision is what happens after the first response. Rather than accepting the draft automatically, the user should identify what is missing, introduce personal knowledge, compare different approaches, and revise the material against the intended purpose. AI works best when it creates a starting point for thinking rather than deciding the entire direction of the work.
Study instruction:
- "Use AI to create three possible starting points for a difficult assignment, then independently develop a preferred direction and explain why it is appropriate."
Key point:
- AI can reduce the friction of getting started.
- A starting draft should stimulate independent thinking, not end it.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on the Productivity Effects of Generative AI
22. Q: What does research on professional writing suggest about AI-assisted productivity?
A: Show answer
A randomized experiment by Shakked Noy and Whitney Zhang examined generative AI use in professional writing tasks. In the published study, participants assigned to use ChatGPT completed their tasks more quickly on average and received higher quality evaluations than participants who did not use it. The findings demonstrate that AI can assist with structured writing work, although the size of the benefit depends on the task, user, and evaluation method. The study does not establish that every AI-generated document is correct or that every profession will experience identical results. Teams should measure their own time savings, quality, error rates, and review effort rather than assuming that productivity improvements transfer universally.
Study instruction:
- "Review the research methods and findings, then design a small workplace trial that measures task time, quality, and correction effort."
Key point:
- Controlled research supports the potential productivity benefits of AI-assisted writing.
- Local testing is necessary before applying research findings to a different workflow.
Source links:
- Science — Noy and Zhang, Experimental Evidence on the Productivity Effects of Generative Artificial Intelligence
23. Q: Why can AI-generated documents be useful for marketing, management, and strategic communication?
A: Show answer
Many professional documents follow recognizable structures and allow some flexibility in tone, organization, and phrasing. AI can quickly propose headlines, outlines, performance-review drafts, strategic memos, grant narratives, and communication alternatives. Users can then focus on audience needs, factual accuracy, organizational context, and the decisions the document must support. However, a polished draft may still misstate facts, overlook sensitive context, or make unsupported claims about people or performance. Final review should therefore examine both the writing quality and the substantive accuracy of the document.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a professional document with AI and review it separately for structure, factual accuracy, audience suitability, tone, and unsupported claims."
Key point:
- AI can reduce drafting effort and improve document organization.
- Professional appearance does not remove the need for content review.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
24. Q: Why can AI be useful for software development even when programming appears highly technical?
A: Show answer
Software development combines pattern recognition with creative problem-solving. AI can help translate a plain-language requirement into a code draft, explain unfamiliar syntax, suggest alternative designs, identify possible errors, and prepare test cases. This can make programming more accessible to people who understand the problem but lack extensive coding experience. Nevertheless, generated code may contain security weaknesses, faulty assumptions, incorrect dependencies, or edge cases that were not considered. Developers should inspect the code, test its behavior, review its security, and understand the consequences before deploying it in a real system.
Study instruction:
- "Ask AI to implement a small function, then test normal inputs, invalid inputs, boundary conditions, and possible security weaknesses."
Key point:
- AI can support coding, explanation, debugging, and experimentation.
- Code that runs successfully is not necessarily secure or correct in every circumstance.

- NIST — Generative AI Profile
25. Q: How can AI summarize complex information without becoming a source of new errors?
A: Show answer
AI can help compress lengthy documents into themes, comparisons, potential risks, and concise explanations. This is useful when the reader needs an overview before examining the underlying evidence. However, summarization can omit qualifications, confuse relationships, misstate quantities, or introduce claims that were not present in the source. A reliable process compares the summary against the original material, checks important numbers and conclusions, and identifies uncertainties explicitly. For consequential work, the reviewer should preserve links to the source passages that support the summary's material claims.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize a report with AI and audit the summary for omitted qualifications, unsupported additions, factual mistakes, and numerical inconsistencies."
Key point:
- Summarization can accelerate analysis and information retrieval.
- Every consequential conclusion must remain traceable to its source.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
26. Q: What ethical and legal concerns arise when AI is used to create images, music, or other artistic work?
A: Show answer
Generative systems can produce material influenced by patterns learned from existing cultural works. This raises questions about training data, attribution, imitation, consent, ownership, and the possible reproduction of protected expression. A user should not assume that an output is legally unrestricted merely because a model created it. Relevant considerations include the nature of the output, the source material, the terms of the AI service, the intended use, and applicable law in the relevant jurisdiction. Organizations should establish review procedures for commercially distributed work and seek appropriate legal advice where the rights position is unclear.
Study instruction:
- "Review the proposed use of an AI-generated creative asset and identify relevant questions concerning source material, licensing, ownership, attribution, and commercial distribution."
Key point:
- Technical ability to generate a work does not settle its legal or ethical status.
- Rights and licensing questions depend on the facts and applicable jurisdiction.
Source links:
- U.S. Copyright Office — Report on Copyrightability of Generative AI Outputs
27. Q: Why should creative professionals exercise caution when asking AI to imitate a specific artist's style?
A: Show answer
Style imitation can raise concerns about creative identity, market competition, and the relationship between a new work and existing artists. A generated work may reproduce recognizable stylistic features even when the user did not intend to copy a particular protected work. Ethical concerns may also arise when a living artist's distinctive approach is used without consent or compensation. A more constructive approach is to describe the desired qualities directly, such as restrained color, geometric composition, dramatic lighting, or a particular historical movement, while assessing whether the result improperly reproduces protected expression. Legal treatment varies, so ethical and legal reviews should remain distinct.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a style-imitation request using descriptive visual characteristics and assess the resulting work for originality, similarity, and appropriate rights clearance."
Key point:
- Broad aesthetic direction can support experimentation without requiring direct imitation.
- Similarity, rights, and ethical concerns require assessment in context.
Source links:
- U.S. Copyright Office — Copyright and Artificial Intelligence
28. Q: Why does subject-matter knowledge make AI-assisted creativity more effective?
A: Show answer
AI can generate possibilities, but users need sufficient knowledge to recognize which possibilities are meaningful, original, misleading, or unsuitable. A person familiar with a field can supply better context, identify subtle differences, challenge superficial suggestions, and request more precise revisions. Cultural and historical knowledge can also provide richer references for writing, visual design, and conceptual work. Without such knowledge, users may settle for familiar, generic output because they lack the vocabulary to ask for something more distinctive. Expertise therefore remains valuable even when AI can produce the initial material quickly.
Study instruction:
- "Take a generic AI-generated creative output and improve it by adding relevant subject knowledge, context, constraints, and more precise evaluation criteria."
Key point:
- Knowledge helps users direct, evaluate, and refine generated ideas.
- AI access does not eliminate the value of domain expertise.
Source links:
- Scientific Reports — Divergent Creativity in Humans and Large Language Models
29. Q: How can writers, designers, and other creative professionals contribute value when AI generates drafts quickly?
A: Show answer
Creative professionals contribute more than the production of an initial draft. They interpret the brief, understand the audience, develop a distinctive concept, select references, judge tone, shape the experience, and recognize when an output fails to serve its purpose. They can use AI to explore alternatives, but their expertise guides the system toward a coherent and distinctive result. Editing also requires deciding what to remove, what to emphasize, and how different elements fit together. These judgment-based activities remain central to producing work that is appropriate, engaging, and aligned with a real objective.
Study instruction:
- "Revise a generic AI-generated draft for a clearly defined audience and explain the professional decisions that materially improve it."
Key point:
- Creative expertise includes direction, selection, contextual judgment, and revision.
- AI-generated material becomes more useful when guided by an informed professional.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
30. Q: How can generative AI broaden access to creative expression?
A: Show answer
AI tools can lower some technical barriers that previously prevented people from expressing an idea. Someone with limited drawing ability can experiment with visual concepts, while a person who struggles with drafting can develop clearer written material. This can help more people communicate their ideas, create prototypes, and participate in work that previously seemed inaccessible. However, reduced barriers do not eliminate the need for learning, editing, originality, or domain understanding. Access to a creative tool should be distinguished from mastery of the discipline and from the ability to produce work that consistently meets professional standards.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a creative task that was previously difficult to perform and evaluate how AI changes access, required skills, and quality control."
Key point:
- AI can make some forms of creative work accessible to more people.
- Access improves opportunity but does not automatically create expertise or quality.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
31. Q: How can AI improve creative learning without preventing people from developing their own skills?
A: Show answer
AI can serve as a practice partner that proposes examples, offers alternative explanations, critiques a draft, or demonstrates a technique. To support learning, users should attempt the task themselves, compare their results with AI suggestions, understand the differences, and revise their work deliberately. If they accept completed answers without engaging in the reasoning, they may gain immediate output while missing the learning opportunity. The most productive approach uses AI to expose alternatives and provide feedback while keeping the learner responsible for understanding the work and developing independent judgment.
Study instruction:
- "Complete a task independently before requesting AI feedback, then document which changes you accepted, rejected, and learned from."
Key point:
- AI can expand practice and feedback opportunities.
- Learning requires active engagement rather than automatic acceptance of answers.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
32. Q: What is the anchoring risk associated with using AI to produce the first draft?
A: Show answer
An initial suggestion can influence how people interpret later options. When AI provides a polished draft immediately, users may become attached to its framing, structure, and assumptions even if they subsequently edit the wording. This can narrow exploration and make alternatives less likely to receive serious consideration. To reduce the risk, users can define their own objectives first, brainstorm independently, request several fundamentally different approaches, or ask AI to critique its initial proposal. These methods make it easier to compare genuine alternatives before committing to a direction.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a project developed from an AI-first draft with one developed after independent brainstorming and identify signs of anchoring."
Key point:
- The first plausible suggestion can shape subsequent decisions.
- Independent alternatives help preserve creative flexibility.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
33. Q: How can overreliance on AI weaken critical thinking and reasoning?
A: Show answer
Critical thinking develops through framing questions, evaluating evidence, comparing explanations, identifying assumptions, and revising conclusions. If a user delegates all these activities to AI, the final output may improve while the user's own understanding grows less. This is especially concerning when the task is intended to develop professional judgment, analytical ability, or subject expertise. A better approach distinguishes assistance from substitution: use AI to challenge an argument or reveal alternatives, but require the learner or professional to explain the conclusion, verify important claims, and retain responsibility for the reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "Complete an analysis with and without AI assistance and compare the quality of the final result, your understanding, and the reasoning you can explain independently."
Key point:
- Faster completion does not guarantee deeper understanding.
- AI should support critical examination rather than eliminate it.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
34. Q: How should human authorship and responsibility be preserved in AI-assisted work?
A: Show answer
Human involvement should extend beyond submitting an initial prompt. A responsible creator establishes the objective, evaluates alternatives, makes substantive decisions, verifies important claims, edits the result, and takes responsibility for the final material. The appropriate level of disclosure depends on the context, relevant policy, contractual terms, publication requirements, and applicable law. For some work, users should also document the AI's contribution and retain drafts that demonstrate meaningful human review. The important distinction is between using AI as an instrument within a considered creative process and submitting unreviewed output as if its content had been independently established.
Study instruction:
- "Create an AI-use record that identifies the system's contribution, significant human revisions, factual checks, and any required disclosure."
Key point:
- Human responsibility remains important even when AI contributes substantial material.
- Authorship, attribution, and disclosure requirements depend on the context.
Source links:
- U.S. Copyright Office — Copyrightability of Generative AI Outputs
35. Q: Why can the apparent quality of a creative document become disconnected from the effort invested in producing it?
A: Show answer
Historically, producing a polished document often required extensive research, organization, editing, and writing. Generative AI can reduce the time needed to produce fluent material, making it harder to infer the effort or depth of thought behind the finished product. A persuasive document may be the result of careful human reasoning, superficial prompting, or some mixture of both. Readers and managers should therefore evaluate important work using evidence of correctness, suitability, reasoning, and results rather than judging effort solely by appearance. When the process itself matters, appropriate records of review and decision-making can provide additional assurance.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two polished documents with different levels of supporting evidence and determine which provides stronger grounds for trust."
Key point:
- Polished presentation is not a dependable measure of underlying effort or reasoning.
- Evaluation should focus on evidence, quality, and the purpose of the work.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
36. Q: How does AI challenge the traditional signaling value of time-consuming work?
A: Show answer
Some tasks communicate commitment because they require a person to devote attention and time to another person's needs. A carefully prepared recommendation, for example, can signal familiarity with a candidate and a willingness to support that person's application. If AI makes the document inexpensive to produce, its polished quality may no longer provide the same evidence of personal attention. Organizations may need other ways to demonstrate commitment, such as specific examples, direct engagement, accountable review, or a clear explanation of the decisions being supported. The broader issue is that the value of a task may include both its deliverable and the human relationship or judgment expressed through the process.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a document whose value partly depends on personal attention and identify how that value could be preserved when AI assists with drafting."
Key point:
- A document can communicate commitment as well as information.
- Automation may require new ways to demonstrate personal judgment and care.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
37. Q: Why can automated communication become meaningless when both senders and recipients rely heavily on AI?
A: Show answer
A communication process can lose its purpose when producing and processing a document become more important than the information or decision it is intended to convey. For example, a report may be generated automatically, distributed to recipients, and summarized by another system without receiving meaningful human attention. The existence of the document then provides little evidence that the underlying issue was understood or acted upon. Organizations should clarify the decision each communication supports, identify its intended reader, and reduce reporting that no longer influences action. Human review remains necessary where accountability, interpretation, or consequential decisions are involved.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recurring report or communication and determine whether it leads to a demonstrable decision, action, learning outcome, or control."
Key point:
- Producing and exchanging documents is not the same as creating value.
- Communication processes should have a clear purpose and meaningful audience engagement.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Risk Management Profile
38. Q: How can organizations preserve meaning and accountability as more creative tasks are automated?
A: Show answer
Organizations should reconsider which parts of a task genuinely create value and which exist primarily because the previous process was slow or difficult. Routine drafting may be automated while human effort shifts toward defining the problem, checking evidence, discussing implications, supporting colleagues, and making consequential decisions. Managers should also examine whether automated outputs are being read, trusted, or acted upon without adequate review. The objective is not to preserve every historical procedure, but to retain the judgment, learning, relationships, and accountability that make the work worthwhile. Any redesigned process should specify its purpose, responsible owner, quality criteria, and review requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Map a work process into automated tasks, human judgment tasks, relationship-based activities, and controls that must remain accountable."
Key point:
- Automation should improve the work rather than merely increase document production.
- Human judgment and responsibility must be deliberately retained where they create value.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Risk Management Profile
39. Q: What is an effective workflow for producing a high-quality creative project with AI?
A: Show answer
Start by defining the objective, audience, constraints, and success criteria. Develop an initial human perspective or a set of alternative directions before asking AI to generate supporting ideas. Use the system to expand possibilities, organize material, or critique a draft, then select the strongest contributions through explicit evaluation. Revise the work using domain knowledge and confirm important factual or legal claims through appropriate evidence. Finally, test the result against the original objective, document significant decisions where needed, and obtain human approval before consequential use. This iterative approach combines rapid generation with deliberate evaluation instead of treating one prompt as a complete production process.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the workflow to a real project and record the objective, alternatives, selection criteria, revisions, verification, and final human decision."
Key point:
- Creative collaboration is an iterative process of generation, evaluation, and refinement.
- Final quality depends on both AI assistance and human decision-making.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
40. Q: How can users test whether an AI-generated creative output actually meets the original brief?
A: Show answer
Translate the brief into explicit acceptance criteria before reviewing the output. These may include relevance to the audience, compliance with constraints, originality, factual accuracy, readability, feasibility, and suitability for the intended use. Compare the output against each criterion rather than relying on a general impression that it looks good. If it fails, identify the exact gap and request a targeted revision instead of regenerating everything without direction. For complex work, use examples, test cases, stakeholder feedback, and independent review to establish whether the result meets practical requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Create an acceptance checklist for a creative deliverable, score the first draft, and document the changes required to meet the brief."
Key point:
- Evaluation criteria make quality more consistent and observable.
- Targeted revision is more controlled than repeated unguided generation.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
41. Q: What role should verification and provenance play in an AI-assisted creative workflow?
A: Show answer
Verification establishes whether important claims and requirements are supported, while provenance records where material came from and how it was developed. Depending on the task, provenance may include the original brief, source documents, research references, AI-generated drafts, human edits, permissions, and approval records. Such documentation is especially useful when work is published, commercially distributed, evaluated by others, or used in a regulated process. Not every informal idea requires an extensive record, but the organization should define the evidence required for each class of work and ensure that users can distinguish verified facts from unconfirmed suggestions.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a provenance checklist for an AI-assisted deliverable and identify which evidence should be retained before publication or approval."
Key point:
- Verification supports accuracy; provenance supports traceability.
- Documentation requirements should reflect the nature and consequences of the work.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
42. Q: How should teams measure whether AI genuinely improves a creative process?
A: Show answer
Measure more than the time required to produce the first draft. Relevant indicators may include total completion time, quality of the final result, diversity of ideas, number of substantive corrections, factual error rates, stakeholder satisfaction, implementation success, and the amount of expert review required. Compare the AI-assisted workflow with a realistic baseline and use similar tasks and evaluation criteria. Record both benefits and new costs, including checking, editing, tool administration, and rework. A process is improved when it achieves a better balance of quality, speed, risk, and usefulness, not simply when it generates material more quickly.
Study instruction:
- "Design a small comparison of AI-assisted and conventional workflows and define measurable indicators for productivity, quality, verification, and rework."
Key point:
- Speed alone is an incomplete measure of productivity.
- Evaluation should account for the whole workflow and the quality of its results.
Source links:
- Science — Experimental Evidence on Generative AI and Professional Writing
43. Q: How should teams handle uncertainty when an AI-generated idea appears valuable but contains unverified assumptions?
A: Show answer
Separate the promising concept from the claims used to justify it. Identify what is known, what is inferred, and what remains uncertain. Then formulate the cheapest reliable test that could resolve the most important uncertainty, such as consulting a primary source, interviewing potential users, building a prototype, or conducting a small experiment. Do not reject every uncertain idea merely because it needs testing, but do not promote a hypothesis into a fact without evidence. A clear record of assumptions and test results allows the team to decide whether to refine, pursue, or abandon the proposal.
Study instruction:
- "Take a promising but uncertain AI-generated proposal and prepare a list of assumptions, evidence gaps, test methods, and decision thresholds."
Key point:
- Uncertainty should be investigated rather than hidden by confident presentation.
- Low-cost experiments can distinguish useful ideas from attractive speculation.
Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Profile
44. Q: What should a responsible organization include in its guidance for AI-assisted creative work?
A: Show answer
Guidance should define approved uses, prohibited or restricted uses, expectations for factual verification, treatment of confidential information, copyright and licensing checks, disclosure obligations, and rules governing final approval. It should distinguish low-risk brainstorming from public-facing or consequential work that requires stronger review. Employees should know who is responsible for checking the output, how errors are reported, and what evidence must be retained. The guidance should also encourage experimentation within appropriate safeguards so that employees can discover useful applications without assuming that every task is suitable for automation.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a practical AI-use checklist for a team and specify the review, evidence, and approval requirements for different risk levels."
Key point:
- Clear guidance makes experimentation more consistent and accountable.
- Controls should match the risks and requirements of the intended use.
Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative AI Profile
45. Q: What is the most useful principle for deciding when to invite AI into creative work?
A: Show answer
Invite AI when it can expand exploration, accelerate a draft, expose alternative perspectives, or help examine a problem, provided the task has suitable safeguards. Do not assume that a fluent response is correct, that a long list is diverse, that a novel combination is original, or that an impressive draft is ready for use. Decide the objective first, provide relevant context, explore alternatives, evaluate the strongest candidates, verify consequential claims, and retain accountability for the result. AI's greatest practical value comes from improving the creative process while allowing human knowledge, judgment, and responsibility to guide the outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Apply a decision checklist to three potential AI uses and explain the expected benefit, principal risk, verification method, and human responsibility for each."
Key point:
- Use AI to expand human capability rather than surrender judgment.
- The appropriate balance depends on the task, the evidence required, and the consequences of error.
Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
Rows: 45
Table 6
AI at Work: Tasks, Collaboration, and Organizational Change
1. Q: How is artificial intelligence likely to affect jobs and professional work?
A: Show answer
AI is likely to affect most occupations because jobs contain a mixture of tasks involving information processing, writing, analysis, communication, creativity, and decision-making. However, exposure to AI does not mean that an entire occupation will disappear. A technology may perform certain tasks effectively while leaving other responsibilities dependent on human judgment, physical activity, trust, accountability, or coordination. The practical question is therefore not simply whether AI can perform a job, but which parts of the job it can perform, how reliably it performs them, and how the surrounding organization responds. Workers and employers should examine changes in task composition, required skills, work quality, and organizational arrangements before drawing conclusions about job replacement. These distinctions help people prepare for changing responsibilities rather than treating automation as an all-or-nothing outcome.
Study instruction:
- Examine one occupation and identify tasks that AI may assist, tasks requiring substantial human involvement, and tasks that are difficult to automate.
Key point:
- AI exposure indicates potential technological influence, not automatic job elimination.
- The effects on employment depend on task composition and the systems surrounding the occupation.
Source links:
- A Method to Link Advances in Artificial Intelligence to Occupational Abilities — American Economic Association
2. Q: Why should a job be understood as a collection of tasks rather than a single responsibility?
A: Show answer
A job title describes a broad role, but actual work consists of activities with different objectives, levels of difficulty, and consequences. A manager, researcher, accountant, or teacher may spend time analyzing information, preparing documents, communicating with colleagues, exercising professional judgment, and handling administrative procedures. AI may support some of these activities without being capable of performing the entire role. Breaking work into tasks makes it possible to determine where AI can improve productivity and where human expertise remains essential. It also helps organizations redesign responsibilities rather than assuming that every task must continue to be performed in the same way. Task-level analysis creates a practical starting point for experimentation, training, and evaluating the consequences of new technology.
Study instruction:
- Decompose a familiar job into at least eight recurring tasks and assess the likely benefits, limitations, and risks of AI assistance for each task.
Key point:
- Jobs are bundles of activities with different automation potential.
- Task analysis is more useful than making decisions from job titles alone.
Source links:
- AI and Occupational Abilities — American Economic Association
3. Q: Why must organizations consider work systems when evaluating the impact of AI on jobs?
A: Show answer
Work takes place within interconnected systems of authority, technology, professional standards, customer relationships, legal obligations, and organizational expectations. Even when AI can perform a task, replacing the human responsible for it may require changes to approval procedures, accountability arrangements, training, customer acceptance, and the responsibilities of other employees. For example, an AI system might prepare an analytical recommendation, but an organization still needs to decide who approves the decision, explains it to stakeholders, and accepts responsibility for its consequences. These dependencies can slow or reshape automation. Organizations should therefore examine not only technical capability but also the institutional arrangements that determine whether a new way of working is practical and acceptable.
Study instruction:
- Map the people, systems, approval stages, and external stakeholders involved in one work process before identifying opportunities for AI automation.
Key point:
- Technical capability alone does not establish organizational feasibility.
- Changes to work often require redesigning responsibilities and supporting systems.
Source links:
- Algorithms at Work: The New Contested Terrain of Control — Academy of Management Annals
4. Q: What is the jagged frontier of AI capabilities, and why does it matter?
A: Show answer
The jagged frontier describes the uneven boundary between tasks AI can perform effectively and tasks for which its assistance may be unreliable or harmful. AI performance does not increase uniformly with apparent task difficulty. A model might produce excellent marketing ideas or summarize a long document while failing on a seemingly straightforward task involving misleading evidence, unusual constraints, or a subtle analytical distinction. This makes it dangerous to assume that success on one activity proves competence on another. The frontier also changes as models, tools, and working methods improve. Individuals should discover where AI works well within their own responsibilities by testing concrete tasks, comparing results with independent evidence, and recording failure patterns. The objective is to make informed decisions about when to use AI, when to verify it, and when to work independently.
Study instruction:
- Build a small task-testing matrix showing where AI improves results, where its contribution is uncertain, and where independent work performs better.
Key point:
- AI capability is uneven across tasks, even within the same profession.
- The frontier must be tested in practice rather than inferred from general impressions.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Harvard Business School AI Institute
5. Q: What does the management-consulting research on AI reveal about productivity and quality?
A: Show answer
The research discussed in the chapter examined consultants completing realistic professional tasks with and without access to GPT-4. For tasks within the model's effective capability frontier, AI-assisted participants completed more work, worked faster, and produced higher-quality results on average. The same research also demonstrated that performance could deteriorate on a task deliberately designed to expose a model limitation. This combination matters more than a simple productivity comparison: AI can create substantial benefits when the task fits its capabilities, yet still encourage errors when people rely on it beyond those capabilities. The findings support selective, evidence-based adoption rather than unrestricted delegation. Organizations should evaluate performance across different types of work, including difficult exceptions, instead of assuming that success on routine tasks establishes reliability throughout a workflow.
Study instruction:
- Compare the benefits and limitations reported in the consulting experiment and explain why the same tool may improve one task while worsening another.
Key point:
- Productivity gains depend on the relationship between task requirements and AI capabilities.
- Average improvements must not conceal failures on important individual tasks.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Organization Science
6. Q: Why can relying too heavily on a capable AI system make a professional perform worse?
A: Show answer
A highly capable system can encourage users to reduce their own effort, accept recommendations without sufficient examination, and overlook weaknesses they would otherwise have detected. This is especially dangerous when an AI answer is polished and plausible but rests on a mistaken assumption or an incorrect interpretation of evidence. Users may become less attentive precisely because previous interactions have been successful. The chapter discusses this failure mode through the idea of falling asleep at the wheel: the human remains nominally responsible but no longer exercises meaningful oversight. Preventing this problem requires more than a final cursory review. Users need to understand the task, identify critical assumptions, examine evidence independently, and retain the ability to recognize when an output is wrong. The amount of review should reflect the consequences of an error and the user's ability to verify the result.
Study instruction:
- Identify three situations in which an accurate-looking AI answer could be wrong and specify how you would independently detect each error.
Key point:
- Confidence and fluency are not proof of correctness.
- Human oversight fails when responsibility remains with the person but critical thinking is abandoned.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Harvard Business School AI Institute
7. Q: How can individuals discover which of their own tasks fall within AI's effective capability frontier?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying recurring tasks and testing AI assistance on representative examples. Establish what a satisfactory human-produced result looks like before evaluating the AI output. Compare accuracy, completeness, time required, quality, and the amount of correction needed. Include ordinary cases and difficult exceptions, because the system may perform well on typical inputs while failing on unusual ones. Record the prompt, model or tool used, reference answer, errors found, and changes made during review. For high-stakes work, test against independently verified evidence and involve an appropriate reviewer. Repeat the assessment as models and workflows change. Over time, this creates a practical understanding of where AI is useful and where its involvement requires additional safeguards or should be avoided.
Study instruction:
- Run a controlled comparison using several representative tasks, preserve the original outputs, and document the criteria used to judge performance.
Key point:
- Test AI against real task requirements and measurable outcomes.
- Reassess the frontier as tools, data, and working conditions change.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Organization Science
8. Q: How can the four task categories help people decide how to use AI at work?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents a practical framework based on four categories. Just Me Tasks remain with the human because AI adds little value or because the person considers human ownership important. Delegated Tasks are assigned to AI, with the person checking the result. Automated Tasks are allowed to proceed without routine human review because the workflow has been shown to be sufficiently reliable and appropriate. Centaur and Cyborg approaches describe different ways of combining human and AI effort across tasks and workflows. The categories help separate the question of whether AI can do something from the question of how much responsibility it should receive. They are not permanent classifications or universal rules. A task's category depends on demonstrated capability, consequences of errors, available safeguards, personal values, and organizational requirements.
Study instruction:
- Classify ten tasks using the framework and document why each task belongs in its category, including what evidence would justify changing that decision.
Key point:
- AI tasks should be allocated deliberately rather than by habit.
- The appropriate level of delegation depends on capability, risk, and human preferences.
9. Q: What makes a task a Just Me Task?
A: Show answer
A Just Me Task is work that a person chooses to retain rather than delegate to AI. There are two important reasons. First, AI may not perform the task well enough to be useful. Second, the person may value human involvement even if AI can produce a technically acceptable result. Personal writing, meaningful relationships, important decisions, and the expression of individual values can belong in this category depending on the circumstances. The classification is therefore not simply a statement about the technical limits of a model. It also reflects what a person considers worthwhile, authentic, or ethically appropriate. As AI improves, tasks previously reserved for humans because of poor AI performance may become candidates for collaboration, while tasks deliberately kept human may remain there because the person values the experience or responsibility itself.
Study instruction:
- Identify three tasks you would retain as human responsibilities and explain whether your reason is limited AI capability, personal preference, ethical concern, or some combination.
Key point:
- A Just Me Task may reflect either practical limitations or human values.
- Better AI performance does not mean every task should be delegated.
10. Q: How should organizations distinguish tasks that AI cannot perform reliably from tasks that should remain human-led?
A: Show answer
These categories require separate judgments. A capability judgment asks whether AI can meet the task's quality, reliability, and contextual requirements. A human-ownership judgment asks whether the organization or individual believes that human involvement is necessary for ethical, relational, legal, or personal reasons. For example, an AI system might help draft a sensitive message, but the responsible person may still need to make the final decision and communicate personally. Conversely, an apparently routine calculation may remain unsuitable for AI delegation if errors cannot be detected before they cause harm. Organizations should record both the technical assessment and the reason for retaining or assigning responsibility. This prevents the mistake of treating technical feasibility as the only consideration in work design.
Study instruction:
- Select one technically automatable task and explain the circumstances under which human ownership should nevertheless be preserved.
Key point:
- Technical capability and acceptable delegation are different decisions.
- Ethical and relational responsibilities should be considered alongside efficiency.
Source links:
- Algorithms at Work — Academy of Management Annals
11. Q: What are Delegated Tasks, and which tasks are appropriate candidates for delegation?
A: Show answer
Delegated Tasks are assigned to AI to save time or reduce effort, while a person retains responsibility for checking the result. They often include repetitive administrative work, initial drafting, document summarization, meeting preparation, and organizing large amounts of information. A suitable candidate is sufficiently well defined that the system can produce a useful result and the human can check it at a reasonable cost. Delegation does not require that the task be simple; complex work can also be delegated when a qualified reviewer can validate the output. Before delegating, specify the expected result, relevant constraints, required evidence, and conditions that require escalation. The objective is to reduce unnecessary effort while preserving quality and accountability, not merely to produce an output faster.
Study instruction:
- Choose a time-consuming task you perform regularly and write a delegation brief covering the objective, inputs, constraints, acceptance criteria, and review procedure.
Key point:
- Good Delegated Tasks save effort while remaining reviewable.
- Clear instructions and acceptance criteria improve the usefulness of AI output.
Source links:
- Experimental Evidence on the Productivity Effects of Generative Artificial Intelligence — Science
12. Q: How should a person verify work delegated to AI without losing the productivity benefit?
A: Show answer
Verification should focus on the parts of the task most likely to produce material errors. Check factual claims against reliable sources, validate calculations independently where necessary, test whether instructions were followed, and examine any assumptions that could change the conclusion. For complex work, break the output into reviewable components and request supporting evidence or intermediate results. Do not assume that a long explanation constitutes proof. Use risk-based review: low-consequence drafts may need a lighter check, while financial decisions, legal documents, personnel decisions, and public communications can require stronger validation and approval. The goal is to develop an efficient control process that catches meaningful errors without recreating all the work the AI was intended to reduce.
Study instruction:
- Design separate verification checklists for a routine email draft and a high-consequence analytical recommendation.
Key point:
- Verification should be proportional to error risk and detectability.
- Review quality matters more than simply reading an output once.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Harvard Business School AI Institute
13. Q: What distinguishes Automated Tasks from Delegated Tasks?
A: Show answer
An Automated Task proceeds with little or no routine human intervention, whereas a Delegated Task still depends on human review. Automation is appropriate only when the process has demonstrated sufficient reliability, the task is well bounded, and errors can be prevented or detected through suitable controls. Spam filtering is an example of a familiar activity that many people already allow AI-enabled systems to perform with limited oversight. By contrast, an unfamiliar system generating consequential financial, legal, or personnel decisions should not be treated as safely automated merely because it performs well in a small demonstration. The organization needs evidence about normal performance, exceptions, failure handling, monitoring, and recovery procedures. Automation should be expanded gradually as the evidence supports it, rather than granted solely on the basis of convenience.
Study instruction:
- Identify the minimum reliability evidence and safeguards required before moving one delegated task into an automated workflow.
Key point:
- Automation removes routine review, so its control requirements are higher.
- Reliability must be demonstrated in the intended operating environment.
14. Q: Why can task categories change as AI capabilities improve?
A: Show answer
The boundary between human work, collaboration, delegation, and automation is dynamic. An activity that currently requires close human review may become sufficiently reliable for greater delegation after model improvements, better data, or stronger validation mechanisms. A task retained by a person because AI performs poorly may eventually become suitable for collaboration. The reverse can also occur: newly discovered failure modes, changed circumstances, or increased consequences may justify reducing automation. Organizations should therefore review classifications periodically and whenever material changes occur to tools, inputs, workflows, or policy. A fixed automation plan can become outdated even when the system continues to operate as designed. The appropriate objective is controlled adaptation supported by evidence, rather than maximizing automation as an end in itself.
Study instruction:
- Create a review trigger list specifying which technology changes, error patterns, or business changes should cause a task's AI classification to be reconsidered.
Key point:
- Task classifications reflect current capability and context, not permanent truths.
- Improvements should lead to reassessment, not automatic unrestricted delegation.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Organization Science
15. Q: What is the Centaur approach to working with AI?
A: Show answer
The Centaur approach creates a deliberate division of labor between a person and an AI system. The human performs activities where personal expertise or judgment is especially valuable, then assigns appropriate parts of the workflow to AI. For example, an analyst might determine which questions to investigate and what conclusions require examination, while the AI helps produce graphs, organize evidence, or draft a preliminary summary. The division can be adjusted to the strengths of each participant rather than forcing both to work on every step. This approach is particularly useful when tasks have identifiable boundaries and the human can determine which components require personal attention. It also encourages professionals to think carefully about work allocation instead of treating AI as a universal substitute or using it without a defined purpose.
Study instruction:
- Redesign one workflow using a two-column responsibility map showing which steps belong to the human and which can be assigned to AI.
Key point:
- Centaur work relies on a strategic division of labor.
- Responsibility should be allocated according to demonstrated strengths and limitations.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Harvard Business School AI Institute
16. Q: What is the Cyborg approach to working with AI?
A: Show answer
The Cyborg approach integrates human and AI effort throughout a workflow rather than separating their responsibilities into distinct blocks. A person might develop an initial argument, ask AI to generate alternative approaches, select promising ideas, revise the work, and request another round of critique. The two participants alternate repeatedly as the task evolves. This method is useful when a person is exploring possibilities, developing ideas, or overcoming a barrier that prevents progress. The AI may contribute options rather than finished content, and its suggestions may influence the human's thinking even when none of the generated wording is retained. Effective Cyborg work still requires the person to set the direction, judge the contributions, and retain control over the final result.
Study instruction:
- Use an AI system iteratively to develop an initial idea, compare several alternatives, critique one selected option, and prepare a final human-reviewed result.
Key point:
- Cyborg work integrates human and AI contributions within the same workflow.
- Iteration can expand possibilities without requiring the human to accept generated output unchanged.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Harvard Business School AI Institute
17. Q: How do Centaur and Cyborg approaches differ, and when is each useful?
A: Show answer
Centaur work emphasizes a relatively clear handoff between human and AI responsibilities. Cyborg work emphasizes continuous interaction, with the human and AI contributing to successive parts of a task. A Centaur arrangement may suit a workflow in which an experienced analyst defines a problem and then delegates data organization to AI. A Cyborg arrangement may be more appropriate when drafting, brainstorming, editing, and evaluating ideas require repeated cycles of interaction. Neither approach is universally superior. The decision depends on task structure, the need for exploration, the reliability of outputs, and the relative strengths of the participants. Both approaches are intended to combine complementary capabilities rather than allow the technology to determine the entire process independently.
Study instruction:
- Choose two different workplace activities and justify whether a structured handoff or iterative collaboration would produce a better result.
Key point:
- Centaurs divide work; Cyborgs intertwine work.
- Task requirements should determine the collaboration model.
Source links:
- Navigating the Jagged Technological Frontier — Harvard Business School AI Institute
18. Q: How can AI help people overcome writing difficulties without replacing their personal voice?
A: Show answer
AI can help a writer overcome an initial blockage by suggesting alternative structures, argument sequences, examples, or tones. It can also identify unclear passages, simplify unnecessarily complex language, and propose different ways to express an idea. The writer does not need to adopt the generated text verbatim for the assistance to be useful. Several alternatives may reveal a direction the writer had not considered, helping the person continue working with renewed clarity. Personal voice remains an important consideration because polished wording alone does not guarantee originality, authenticity, or alignment with the author's intended meaning. A practical approach is to use AI as a source of options and critique, then make deliberate choices about what to retain and how to revise it.
Study instruction:
- Take a paragraph that is difficult to write, request several structurally different alternatives, and document which ideas you retain and why.
Key point:
- AI can reduce creative friction without becoming the final author.
- Human selection and revision preserve purpose, meaning, and individual judgment.
Source links:
- Experimental Evidence on the Productivity Effects of Generative Artificial Intelligence — Science
19. Q: How can AI support reading, research, and the interpretation of technical material?
A: Show answer
AI can make complex material more accessible by producing preliminary summaries, organizing arguments, explaining terminology, and identifying questions for further investigation. It may be particularly useful when a reader understands the subject broadly but finds a technical paper difficult to navigate. However, summarization is not the same as independent verification. The system can omit qualifications, misinterpret methods, or overstate conclusions. A strong workflow uses the summary to guide reading rather than replace it: check important claims against the original source, inspect methods and evidence, distinguish the authors' findings from AI-generated interpretation, and preserve references to relevant passages. This makes AI a support for comprehension while allowing the human reader to retain responsibility for interpretation.
Study instruction:
- Summarize a technical paper with AI, then compare the summary against the paper's research question, methods, limitations, and conclusions.
Key point:
- Summaries can accelerate orientation and understanding.
- Important findings and interpretations must be checked against the original evidence.
20. Q: How can simulated AI reviewers help improve professional work?
A: Show answer
An AI system can be asked to evaluate a draft from several defined perspectives. A critical reviewer might look for weak arguments, a creative collaborator might suggest alternative examples, and a general reader might identify confusing explanations. These perspectives help the author examine the work from angles that are easy to overlook when deeply involved in its creation. Specific role instructions are more useful than simply asking for general feedback because they direct attention toward particular weaknesses.
