- Khanmigo for Educators — Khan Academy
- AI Competency Framework for Teachers — UNESCO
25. Q: How should educators use AI-generated feedback without allowing it to replace professional evaluation?
A: Show answer
AI can provide preliminary comments on structure, clarity, missing steps, practice results, and possible misunderstandings. This can help students receive feedback more frequently than would be possible through teacher review alone. However, automated feedback may misunderstand the assignment, overlook acceptable alternative approaches, or reinforce errors. Teachers should determine when automated feedback is suitable, which work requires human judgment, and what evidence is needed to resolve disagreements. High-stakes assessments, sensitive personal circumstances, and complex performance judgments deserve particular care. Students should also have a route to question or correct misleading feedback. Educators can improve the process by using clear rubrics, representative test examples, and regular checks of whether automated feedback agrees with expert evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Compare AI feedback with teacher feedback on the same sample of student work and categorize disagreements as factual, interpretive, rubric-related, or substantive.
Key point:
- AI feedback can increase responsiveness but is not automatically reliable.
- Educators must retain responsibility for consequential judgments.
Source links:
- Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning — U.S. Department of Education
- Khanmigo for Educators — Khan Academy
26. Q: What privacy and safeguarding requirements should schools consider before introducing AI tutors?
A: Show answer
Schools should determine what personal information a tool collects, why the information is needed, how long it is retained, who can access it, and whether it is used for purposes beyond education. Student work, conversations, performance information, disability information, and family circumstances can be sensitive. Schools should assess the provider's contractual terms, security arrangements, data-processing practices, age requirements, and applicable privacy laws before adoption. Access should be limited to authorized users, and unnecessary personal information should not be entered into public or unapproved services. Younger learners may require parent or guardian involvement and additional supervision. Schools should also explain monitoring and data use in understandable terms. Privacy and safeguarding are ongoing responsibilities, not checks completed once when a product is purchased.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a privacy and safeguarding checklist covering data collected, purpose, access, retention, deletion, vendor terms, age restrictions, and incident response.
Key point:
- Educational usefulness does not remove privacy or child-safety obligations.
- Data collection should be necessary, transparent, proportionate, and governed.
Source links:
- Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research — UNESCO
- AI and Education: Protecting the Rights of Learners — UNESCO
27. Q: How can schools assess and reduce bias or unfairness in AI-supported learning?
A: Show answer
AI systems can perform differently across languages, cultural contexts, disabilities, socioeconomic groups, and levels of subject knowledge. Their training data and design choices can affect which responses appear normal, whose perspectives are represented, and which mistakes are detected. Schools should examine outputs using varied examples and determine whether different learners receive consistently useful explanations and feedback. They should provide accessible ways to report problems and avoid using unvalidated AI scores as the sole basis for important educational decisions. Human reviewers must consider whether a system's recommendation reflects a genuine learning need or an unexamined assumption. Fairness requires ongoing monitoring because performance can change as tools, learners, and curricula change. No single test eliminates every form of bias, but structured evaluation can identify preventable problems before they cause harm.
Study instruction:
- Test the same educational task across different language levels and student contexts, then document differences in accuracy, tone, accessibility, and recommendations.
Key point:
- AI performance should be assessed across the populations it will serve.
- Human oversight and mechanisms for challenging decisions are essential safeguards.
Source links:
- AI and Education: Protecting the Rights of Learners — UNESCO
- Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning — U.S. Department of Education
28. Q: How could AI tutoring reduce global educational inequality, and why is access alone insufficient?
A: Show answer
AI tutoring could extend access to explanations, practice, and individualized assistance in communities where specialist teachers or supplementary educational resources are scarce. Digital delivery may reduce some geographic and financial barriers to educational support. However, the benefits depend on reliable connectivity, appropriate devices, accessible interfaces, suitable language support, relevant curricula, and the ability to use the system safely. Schools also need teachers who can integrate the technology and help learners interpret its outputs. Unequal access can widen existing gaps if better-resourced students benefit first while others face infrastructure or accessibility barriers. Governments and educational institutions should therefore consider the total cost of adoption, offline or low-bandwidth alternatives, local-language performance, and support for disadvantaged learners. Equitable implementation requires deliberate design and evidence, not simply distributing accounts.
Study instruction:
- Assess a proposed AI tutoring program against device access, connectivity, language, disability access, total cost, teacher support, and evidence of learning impact.
Key point:
- AI may expand learning opportunities, but infrastructure and institutional support determine who benefits.
- Equity must be evaluated explicitly.
Source links:
- AI and Education: Protecting the Rights of Learners — UNESCO
- Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research — UNESCO
29. Q: How should a school evaluate whether an AI tutoring system actually improves learning?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should begin with explicit objectives, such as improved conceptual understanding, retention, independent problem-solving, engagement, or reduced time to mastery. Schools should establish baseline measures and compare outcomes with appropriate alternatives, accounting for differences in student populations, teaching methods, and available support. Immediate quiz scores alone may not reveal whether students can recall knowledge or apply it after assistance is removed. Evaluation should include independent assessments, delayed retention checks, qualitative feedback, accessibility measures, and possible negative effects such as distraction or excessive reliance. Results should be examined across relevant student groups rather than relying only on an overall average. Costs and teacher workload also matter when considering whether the benefits justify sustained use. Decisions to expand a program should follow evidence against predefined success criteria.
Study instruction:
- Develop an evaluation plan containing baseline measures, comparison conditions, independent assessments, delayed testing, subgroup analysis, cost measures, and stop-or-revise criteria.
Key point:
- Educational effectiveness must be demonstrated, not inferred from demonstrations or user satisfaction.
- Independent and longer-term learning measures are important.

- Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning — U.S. Department of Education
- The 2 Sigma Problem — Benjamin S. Bloom, ERIC
30. Q: What should an institutional policy on student AI use contain?
A: Show answer
A school policy should define acceptable, restricted, and prohibited uses for different assignments and assessment settings. It should explain disclosure expectations, authorship responsibilities, verification requirements, privacy protections, age-related safeguards, consequences for misconduct, and procedures for resolving disputes. Students and educators need concrete examples because broad statements about responsible use can be interpreted inconsistently. Policies should distinguish ordinary learning support from assistance that undermines the objective of an assessment. They should also account for accessibility accommodations and avoid penalizing students simply for using approved tools appropriately. Teachers need institutional support to enforce the rules consistently, while students should have a reasonable opportunity to explain disputed work. The policy must be revisited as tools, instructional practices, and relevant regulations change.
Study instruction:
- Draft an assignment-level AI policy that identifies allowed assistance, prohibited assistance, disclosure requirements, verification duties, and a fair review procedure.
Key point:
- Rules must be clear, practical, and aligned with learning objectives.
- Consistent enforcement and fair procedures support trust.

- Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research — UNESCO
- Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning — U.S. Department of Education
31. Q: How should schools balance AI access for younger learners with age-appropriate safeguards?
A: Show answer
Younger learners may benefit from additional explanations and guided practice, but they also require safeguards suited to their cognitive development, privacy needs, and ability to evaluate unreliable information. Schools should verify minimum-age requirements, applicable law, provider conditions, parent or guardian consent requirements, and the level of adult supervision needed. Tools should be assessed for inappropriate content, misleading instructions, manipulation risks, and unnecessary personal-data collection. Educators should explain when AI is being used and teach children to seek help from trusted adults when a response seems confusing or concerning. Access should not depend solely on a tool's claim to be child-friendly. Institutional decisions should consider actual functionality, testing, monitoring, and available reporting procedures.
Study instruction:
- Create a safeguarding plan for a primary-school AI tutoring pilot covering age eligibility, adult supervision, content filtering, data protection, and incident reporting.
Key point:
- Age-appropriate design and supervision are prerequisites for responsible use.
- Parent, school, and provider responsibilities should be clearly established.

32. Q: How can teachers preserve collaboration and social development when AI provides individualized assistance?
A: Show answer
Individual tutoring can help students address personal learning gaps, but education also develops communication, empathy, cooperation, conflict resolution, and the ability to learn from other people. If every difficulty is handled privately through an AI interface, students may lose opportunities to explain their reasoning, listen to alternative perspectives, and build relationships with classmates. Teachers can combine individual AI-assisted preparation with group investigations, peer teaching, debates, practical projects, and shared problem-solving. Students might use AI to explore possible approaches independently and then compare, challenge, and defend those approaches with peers. The teacher facilitates discussion and helps learners distinguish useful disagreement from unsupported claims. Educational technology should enhance the quality of interpersonal learning rather than displace it.
Study instruction:
- Develop a lesson that combines individual AI-assisted preparation with a collaborative task requiring students to compare evidence and justify a shared conclusion.
Key point:
- Personalized learning and social learning serve complementary purposes.
- Classroom interaction remains a central educational resource.

33. Q: How can schools use AI to support teacher development and reduce routine workload responsibly?
A: Show answer
AI can assist with lesson planning, differentiated examples, question generation, rubric drafting, resource organization, and preliminary analysis of student work. These capabilities may reduce repetitive administrative tasks and make it easier for teachers to explore alternative teaching approaches. Schools should distinguish genuine workload reduction from tasks that simply create more review, monitoring, and reporting. Teachers need opportunities to evaluate outputs, exchange successful practices, and identify failure modes. Professional development should cover instructional design, limitations of AI systems, privacy, bias, verification, and classroom policies rather than focusing only on tool operation. Administrators should measure how the technology affects preparation time, instructional quality, teacher autonomy, and student outcomes. The purpose is to improve professional capacity while retaining the educator's authority and responsibility.
Study instruction:
- Select three routine teacher tasks for an AI-assisted pilot and measure time saved, revision effort, output quality, privacy risks, and classroom impact.
Key point:
- Automation should free capacity for valuable educational work.
- Effective adoption requires training, review, and sustained institutional support.

34. Q: How can educators develop students' metacognition and self-regulated learning through AI tutoring?
A: Show answer
Metacognition involves understanding and monitoring one's own thinking, while self-regulated learning includes setting goals, planning, monitoring progress, and adjusting strategies. An AI tutor can support these abilities by asking students to explain their approach, estimate confidence, identify confusing steps, compare strategies, and reflect on the outcome. However, the system should not complete every stage of reflection on the student's behalf. Learners need to make and review their own decisions. Teachers can incorporate learning logs, confidence judgments, error analyses, and short reflections on when AI assistance was useful. These activities help students become more aware of the difference between recognizing an explanation and mastering a concept. Over time, students should learn to choose appropriate strategies independently and evaluate whether those strategies are working.
Study instruction:
- Create a self-regulated learning worksheet covering goals, initial confidence, strategy selection, use of assistance, error correction, and final reflection.
Key point:
- AI can help learners monitor their thinking, but reflection must remain an active student responsibility.
- Independent learning strategies are transferable beyond a single application.

35. Q: What common mistakes can undermine the effectiveness of AI-assisted learning?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include accepting an answer without verification, asking AI to complete all practice, using a tutor without a defined learning objective, treating generated feedback as authoritative, and confusing task completion with mastery. Teachers may also adopt tools because they are impressive rather than because they address a demonstrated educational need. Other problems include exposing sensitive student information, overlooking accessibility, assuming that all students have equal access, and relying on unvalidated automated scores. These failures are best prevented through clear objectives, explicit use policies, independent checks, teacher training, and regular evaluation. Schools should also collect examples of incorrect or harmful outputs and make it possible for staff and learners to report them. A realistic approach acknowledges the technology's benefits while building controls around its limitations.
Study instruction:
- Review a proposed AI classroom activity and identify at least five failure modes, preventive controls, evidence requirements, and corrective actions.
Key point:
- Most educational risks arise from the interaction between technology, users, and institutional practice.
- Prevention requires both sound instructional design and appropriate governance.

- Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning — U.S. Department of Education
36. Q: How should teachers respond when an AI tutor provides a wrong explanation or inappropriate recommendation?
A: Show answer
The teacher should first identify what is incorrect, misleading, or unsuitable and assess whether the response has affected the learner's understanding or safety. The response may need to be corrected directly, explained against authoritative material, and followed by a new exercise that confirms the learner understands the underlying concept. If the problem involves recurring model behavior, inappropriate content, privacy, or possible bias, the incident should be documented and reported through the school's established process. Teachers should not assume that a tool will become reliable simply because the same request is repeated. Where a tool repeatedly produces serious errors, its use should be restricted or suspended until the problem is assessed. Students should learn to question automated outputs and seek human clarification rather than quietly copying an incorrect answer.
Study instruction:
- Simulate a tutoring failure and document detection, correction, student follow-up, escalation, and the decision about continued use.
Key point:
- Errors require correction at both the learning level and, when necessary, the system level.
- Repeated or serious failures should trigger formal review.

- Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research — UNESCO
- Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning — U.S. Department of Education
37. Q: How can schools preserve foundational literacy, numeracy, and independent problem-solving while allowing AI use?
A: Show answer
Students need foundational skills to communicate, reason quantitatively, evaluate information, and participate meaningfully in more advanced learning. AI can assist with practice and explanation, but it should not routinely perform the very operations students are expected to master. Schools can use a mix of AI-assisted learning activities, supervised practice, independent writing, mental or written calculations, and authentic problem-solving. The proportion of independent work should reflect the learning objective and the learner's stage of development. Teachers can gradually introduce assistance as competence grows, then assess whether students can perform independently when needed. This is not a universal prohibition on AI. It is a deliberate distinction between using technology to extend capability and outsourcing a foundational skill before it has been developed.
Study instruction:
- Identify foundational skills in one course and design a progression from unaided practice through guided AI assistance to independent application.
Key point:
- Foundational capabilities remain necessary in an environment rich in automated assistance.
- AI permissions should depend on the skill being developed or assessed.

38. Q: How can parents support responsible AI use without taking control away from teachers or students?
A: Show answer
Parents can help children establish appropriate boundaries, discuss how AI responses should be checked, and encourage the child to explain concepts in their own words. They can also reinforce the importance of privacy, honesty, independent effort, and seeking adult help when an AI response seems inappropriate. Schools should communicate assignment rules and safeguarding practices so families understand when AI assistance is allowed and when independent work is required. Parents should avoid treating polished AI-generated homework as proof of mastery or pressuring children to use a tool merely to improve visible performance. Their role is to support learning habits and communicate concerns to educators, while teachers remain responsible for instructional design and assessment. Effective cooperation requires shared expectations about learning outcomes, safety, and appropriate technology use.
Study instruction:
- Draft a parent-school communication note explaining approved uses, independent-work expectations, privacy safeguards, and how concerns should be reported.
Key point:
- Families and schools should reinforce consistent learning and safety expectations.
- Student independence and honest effort should remain central.

39. Q: What criteria should administrators use when selecting an AI tutoring product for a school or district?
A: Show answer
Administrators should evaluate pedagogical quality, curriculum alignment, factual reliability, accessibility, privacy, safeguarding, cost, interoperability, and evidence of learning effectiveness. The assessment should examine whether teachers can inspect and override the system's recommendations and whether students can report errors. Institutions should review data-retention terms, age restrictions, security, vendor support, integration requirements, and ongoing costs rather than relying on a demonstration or sales claim. A pilot should use clear success criteria, representative learners, baseline measures, and a process for reporting problems. Administrators should also examine whether the product creates additional burdens through monitoring, corrections, or complex workflows. Procurement decisions should compare educational benefit with financial, operational, and ethical risks.

40. Q: How should schools measure whether AI adoption improves teacher workload and classroom quality?
A: Show answer
Schools should measure both the time saved on individual tasks and the total effort required to use the system responsibly. A tool may generate materials quickly but create additional review, correction, documentation, or troubleshooting work. Evaluation should therefore include preparation time, revision time, classroom engagement, quality of feedback, teacher autonomy, student outcomes, and any unintended consequences. Teachers should be asked which tasks improved and which became more difficult. A comparison with the previous process helps determine whether a reported efficiency gain reflects a real improvement. Benefits should be evaluated over sufficient time to capture adaptation and ongoing maintenance. Administrators should be prepared to revise or discontinue tools that increase workload without corresponding educational gains.
Study instruction:
- Track the time and quality of a recurring teaching task before and after AI introduction, separating generation time, review time, correction time, and follow-up effort.
Key point:
- Faster content production does not automatically mean lower workload.
- Workload claims should be measured alongside instructional outcomes.

41. Q: How can AI tutoring be integrated into a curriculum without creating fragmented or inconsistent learning experiences?
A: Show answer
Integration should begin with curriculum objectives, prerequisite knowledge, and the sequence in which learners develop increasingly advanced competencies. AI activities must reinforce that sequence rather than introduce disconnected explanations or conflicting terminology. Teachers should agree on approved tools, expectations for independent work, methods for checking answers, and the learning evidence required for each stage. Shared materials and evaluation criteria help maintain consistency across classes while allowing appropriate adaptation for individual learners. Schools should also coordinate AI use across subjects to avoid overwhelming students with incompatible requirements or duplicate applications. Curriculum leaders need a process for reviewing tool-generated resources when content or institutional requirements change. Coherence is achieved when AI supports the broader educational program instead of operating as a separate layer with unrelated objectives.
Study instruction:
- Map one unit's objectives, prerequisite knowledge, AI-assisted activities, independent assessments, and teacher review points in the order students will encounter them.
Key point:
- AI activities should align with the curriculum's learning progression.
- Shared expectations help maintain consistency without preventing useful personalization.

42. Q: How should a teacher handle a student who submits an excellent AI-assisted answer but cannot explain it independently?
A: Show answer
The teacher should distinguish the quality of the submitted product from the student's demonstrated understanding. An appropriate follow-up may involve an oral explanation, a similar problem, a short supervised task, or questions about the evidence and decisions behind the answer. The objective is to establish what the student knows, what remains unclear, and whether the work complied with the assignment's rules. The teacher should not automatically assume misconduct because the student cannot immediately explain every detail, since anxiety, communication differences, and other factors can affect performance. Instead, the teacher should gather relevant evidence, review the stated AI-use policy, and provide a fair opportunity for clarification. If unauthorized assistance is established, the institution should follow its documented academic-integrity process and ensure that the student understands how to improve.
Study instruction:
- Create a follow-up assessment that tests the underlying skill independently and separates evidence of misunderstanding from evidence relevant to an academic-integrity review.
Key point:
- A polished submission does not by itself establish independent mastery.
- Verification should be fair, objective, and consistent with published policy.

43. Q: How can an AI tutoring program be piloted and improved before full implementation?
A: Show answer
A pilot should address a clearly identified learning problem and define what evidence would justify continuation. The institution should select a manageable group of teachers and learners, prepare approved activities, establish safeguards, and record baseline performance. During implementation, participants should document errors, confusing interactions, accessibility issues, time demands, and differences in student engagement. Evaluation should compare outcomes with an appropriate alternative where feasible and should include independent measures of learning. Staff and students need a straightforward way to report problems without penalty. At the end of the pilot, leaders should assess educational benefit, cost, risk, teacher workload, and equity before choosing whether to expand, modify, or discontinue the intervention. A pilot is a structured evaluation, not merely a trial period intended to demonstrate success.
Study instruction:
- Write a pilot plan specifying objectives, participants, baseline measures, success thresholds, safeguards, incident reporting, evaluation dates, and the final adoption decision.
Key point:
- Small-scale testing can reveal implementation problems before they affect an entire institution.
- Continuation should depend on predefined evidence and safeguards.

44. Q: What would an effective AI-supported tutoring session look like from beginning to end?
A: Show answer
The session begins with a clear objective and a short diagnostic activity that reveals the learner's starting point. The tutor then presents an explanation or practice task at an appropriate level and asks the student to attempt the work. If the learner struggles, the system provides targeted questions, graduated hints, or alternative explanations rather than automatically completing the task. The learner explains the reasoning, checks the result, and corrects mistakes using suitable evidence. The session concludes with an independent question that tests whether the student can apply the concept without assistance. The teacher reviews important difficulties and decides whether further intervention is necessary. Records should capture only information needed to support the learning process and should be managed according to applicable privacy requirements.
Study instruction:
- Design a complete tutoring-session script with an objective, diagnostic question, guided practice, feedback, independent assessment, and teacher follow-up.
Key point:
- A tutoring session should move from diagnosis through guided practice to independent application.
- The final assessment checks whether assistance has translated into learning.

45. Q: How can educators balance experimentation with AI and the responsibility to protect educational quality?
A: Show answer
AI offers opportunities to develop new activities, personalized practice, simulations, and approaches to feedback that may not have been practical previously. Experimentation is valuable because existing teaching methods should not be assumed to be optimal merely because they are familiar. However, experimentation should remain tied to educational objectives, appropriate safeguards, and observable evidence. Teachers and administrators should identify the risks of each intervention, establish acceptable-use boundaries, and monitor whether the activity improves learning. A creative classroom exercise can be revised or discontinued if it creates confusion, exposes student data, or weakens core skills. Institutions should encourage teachers to share lessons from successful and unsuccessful trials so that evidence can inform future practice. Responsible innovation combines ambition with accountability rather than treating every technological possibility as an automatic improvement.
Study instruction:
- Propose one ambitious AI-based classroom activity and evaluate its educational objective, likely benefits, failure modes, safeguards, and criteria for revision.
Key point:
- Educational innovation should expand possibilities without weakening learning quality or student protection.
- Evidence and reflection turn experimentation into sustainable improvement.

46. Q: What indicators can demonstrate that students are becoming more independent through AI-assisted education?
A: Show answer
Independence can be evaluated by examining whether students solve unfamiliar problems, explain concepts accurately, detect errors, and transfer knowledge without continuous assistance. Additional indicators include the ability to select appropriate strategies, recognize limitations, ask purposeful questions, and determine when external verification is necessary. Schools can compare performance before and after guided AI use, using independent tasks and delayed assessments rather than relying exclusively on activity logs or self-reported confidence. Results should be interpreted in context because a student's level of assistance may appropriately vary with task difficulty and prior experience. Teachers should also look for signs of dependency, such as inability to begin a task without the system or uncritical acceptance of its output. The objective is not to eliminate assistance but to develop the judgment needed to use it selectively and effectively.
Study instruction:
- Design a rubric measuring unaided performance, explanation quality, error detection, strategy selection, transfer, and appropriate use of external assistance.
Key point:
- Independence is demonstrated through performance and judgment, not merely reduced tool usage.
- Evaluation should distinguish productive assistance from harmful dependence.

47. Q: How might AI change the relationship between learning, assessment, and the development of expertise?
A: Show answer
When AI can generate many outputs that previously demonstrated a learner's skill, educators must place greater emphasis on understanding how the outputs were produced and whether the learner can evaluate them. Assessment may increasingly combine independent demonstration, practical application, explanation, critique, collaboration, and responsible use of tools. This change does not make traditional knowledge or basic skills irrelevant. Instead, it increases the importance of knowing when automation is appropriate and how to check its results. Expertise includes the ability to recognize good work, identify important assumptions, understand consequences, and exercise judgment when evidence is incomplete. Educational institutions will need to reconsider task design and assessment practices as AI capabilities evolve. The enduring objective is to develop people who can use tools intelligently while retaining the knowledge and judgment necessary to direct them.
Study instruction:
- Select one professional skill and identify which components require foundational knowledge, which can be supported by AI, and which require independent human judgment.
Key point:
- AI changes how competence is demonstrated but does not eliminate the need for expertise.
- Assessment should capture understanding, judgment, and appropriate tool use.

- AI Competency Framework for Teachers — UNESCO
- Artificial Intelligence and the Future of Teaching and Learning — U.S. Department of Education
48. Q: How can educational institutions ensure that AI expands opportunity while preserving human agency and educational purpose?
A: Show answer
Institutions should define what education is meant to achieve before deciding which activities to automate. AI adoption should improve students' ability to think, understand, communicate, collaborate, and act responsibly rather than simply increase the volume of generated work. Policies should protect student rights, provide meaningful access, preserve teacher judgment, and make consequential automated recommendations open to inspection and correction. Schools should also examine whether tools reinforce inequality, create excessive surveillance, or encourage dependence. Students and educators should have opportunities to participate in decisions about how technology is introduced and evaluated. Progress should be judged through evidence of learning, fairness, safety, and practical value. The long-term purpose is to use technology to develop human capabilities, expand educational opportunity, and support better teaching rather than make education subordinate to technological systems.
Study instruction:
- Draft a school-level AI principles statement connecting learning objectives, human responsibility, student rights, equity, transparency, and evaluation requirements.
Key point:
- Educational purpose must determine how AI is designed, selected, and used.
- Human agency, fairness, and meaningful learning should guide implementation decisions.
